Unit 1 · Foundations of Nursing Care for Adult Clients · Chapter 1

Health, Wellness, and Illness

Health and wellness are individualized and dynamic. In this chapter, the nurse connects a client's usual functioning, modifiable risks, environment, health beliefs, and support systems to practical health-promotion planning.

TL;DR - Wellness Is Individualized

Do not judge wellness by diagnosis alone. Compare the client with the client's usual baseline, identify modifiable risks, assess barriers to adherence, and build health education around the client's beliefs, resources, and support system.

Priority Lens

  • Health and wellness include physical function plus emotional and social contentment.
  • Illness is the client's altered functioning; disease is the physiologic alteration.
  • The illness-wellness continuum centers on the client's normal state of health.

Common Test Move

  • Choose interventions that strengthen coping, activate support, improve education, and reduce barriers.
  • Classify risks as modifiable or nonmodifiable before planning education.
  • Look for denial, mistrust, cultural mismatch, finances, or weak support as adherence barriers.

High-Yield Framework

Core Concepts

Foundations
TermHow to Think About ItNursing Focus
Health/wellnessOptimal functioning and adaptation within the client's own life situation.Ask what "normal" and "well" mean for this client.
IllnessAltered functioning in response to a health problem.Assess how the client perceives the change and how daily roles are affected.
DiseasePhysiologic alteration in body structure or function.Do not assume the disease label predicts the client's wellness level.
ContinuumA way to compare severe illness, usual baseline, and optimal wellness.Set goals that move the client toward the best achievable wellness state.

Variables That Shape Wellness

Assess first

Modifiable

  • Tobacco or other health-risk behaviors.
  • Nutrition, weight, activity, and exercise patterns.
  • Access to health education and preventive care.
  • Sexual practices and other behavior-linked risks.

Nonmodifiable

  • Age, sex, developmental level, and genetic traits.
  • Some inherited or lifelong conditions.
  • Past life experiences that influence current beliefs.
  • Current baseline when a chronic condition is stable.
NCLEX habit: education works best when it targets what the client can actually change. Do not spend the priority intervention on a fixed trait when a modifiable risk is present.

Health/Wellness Assessment

Adherence

ATI frames health promotion around assessment plus barrier reduction. Before teaching, determine what the client believes, what resources exist, and what is blocking follow-through.

  • Complete a focused physical assessment and compare findings with the client's usual state.
  • Ask how the client perceives the illness, its severity, and the prescribed plan.
  • Identify access barriers: cost, transportation, health care availability, and education needs.
  • Assess trust in the provider, belief in the therapy, cultural or religious fit, family roles, and support systems.
  • Look for financial restrictions that can force the client to prioritize one health need over another.

Nursing Actions That Promote Wellness

Intervene
  • Provide resources that strengthen coping, such as dietitian referral, support groups, or practical home tools.
  • With permission, involve the client's support system during illness and stress.
  • Identify obstacles to health and create realistic strategies to reduce them.
  • Use education methods that increase awareness and reduce risk.
  • Improve adherence with concrete supports: written materials, medication organizers, and follow-up planning.

Active Learning Scenario

Basic Concept - Smoking With Cardiovascular Family History

ATI-supported ALS

A clinic client continues to smoke after several quit attempts and has a family history of cardiovascular disease. Build the Basic Concept template around reducing obstacles and strengthening health-promotion behaviors.

Template PartStudy Fill-In
Related contentIdentify barriers to adherence and health promotion, including stress, coping, support, beliefs, access, and prior experiences.
Underlying principleHealth promotion and disease prevention improve when nursing care addresses the client's real barriers, not just the risk label.
Nursing interventionsOffer coping resources, encourage support systems, identify obstacles, build risk-reduction education, and create practical adherence strategies.

Application Exercises

1

A client with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes says meal planning is difficult and prescribed medications are easy to forget. Which actions support adherence? Select all that apply.

  • A. Ask a dietitian to assist with meal planning.
  • B. With consent, involve the client's support system.
  • C. Focus only on age-related cognitive changes.
  • D. Encourage use of a daily medication dispenser.
  • E. Provide written home education materials.
A, B, D, E These actions reduce barriers, strengthen coping/support, and make the plan easier to follow.
C Cognitive assessment can matter, but it is not the best adherence intervention in this scenario.
2

Which client best represents the center of the illness-wellness continuum?

  • A. A student with influenza.
  • B. An older adult with a new diabetes diagnosis.
  • C. A postpartum client with a urinary tract infection.
  • D. A client with long-standing rheumatoid arthritis that is well controlled.
D The center reflects the client's usual state. A stable chronic condition can be the client's normal baseline.
3

At a health fair, which variables should the nurse treat as modifiable? Select all that apply.

  • A. Social smoking.
  • B. BMI of 28.
  • C. Genetic alopecia.
  • D. Trisomy 21.
  • E. Reflux symptoms that can improve with treatment and lifestyle changes.
A, B, E These are realistic targets for education and behavior or treatment planning.
C, D Genetic or chromosomal traits are nonmodifiable.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

Basic Concept: health promotion, wellness variables, adherence barriers, and smoking risk reduction. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after the Templates page receives source-supported examples.

Unit 1 · Foundations of Nursing Care for Adult Clients · Chapter 2

Emergency Nursing Principles and Management

Emergency nursing starts with rapid recognition, triage, and a systematic primary survey. The nurse prioritizes life-threatening problems first, communicates deterioration early, and follows emergency protocols for toxic, thermal, and cardiac events.

TL;DR - ABCDE Before Everything Else

Use triage to sort urgency, then use the ABCDE primary survey to find threats to life: airway/cervical spine, breathing, circulation, disability, and exposure/environment. Call rapid response for signs of sudden decline and follow BLS/ACLS-based protocols for arrest rhythms.

Immediate Priorities

  • Airway is first; without oxygenation, the rest of the survey cannot help.
  • Use jaw-thrust when trauma or cervical spine injury is suspected.
  • Control visible bleeding, obtain large-bore IV access, and replace volume as ordered.

Exam Traps

  • Do not induce vomiting or use ipecac for ingested toxins.
  • Asystole is not a shockable rhythm; prioritize CPR, IV access, epinephrine per protocol, and reversible causes.
  • Use SBAR when activating or documenting a rapid response event.

Emergency Framework

Five-Level ED Triage

Sort first
LevelMeaningExamples
1 - ResuscitationImmediate treatment is needed to prevent death.Cardiac arrest, stroke, pulmonary embolus, overdose.
2 - EmergentTime-sensitive problem could become life- or limb-threatening.Traumatic amputation, head/neck injury, venomous bite.
3 - UrgentTreatment is needed, but the problem is not immediately life-threatening.Kidney stone, gallbladder colic, fracture.
4 - Less urgentThe client can wait for a period of time without immediate treatment.Bladder infection, laceration, infected toe.
5 - NonurgentSimple evaluation and minor care management are expected.Rash, minor cut, backache.

Primary Survey: ABCDE

Life threats
StepAssessPriority Nursing Move
A - Airway/cervical spineResponsiveness, obstruction, blood, vomitus, broken teeth, secretions, trauma risk.Open airway. Use head-tilt/chin-lift only when trauma is not suspected; use modified jaw-thrust with suspected cervical spine injury.
B - BreathingBreath sounds, chest expansion, respiratory effort, rate, depth, oxygenation.Support ventilation with oxygen, bag-valve mask, or advanced airway support as indicated.
C - CirculationHeart rate, blood pressure, pulses, capillary refill, bleeding, shock risk.Start CPR if needed, control hemorrhage, establish large-bore IV access, give isotonic fluids or blood products as ordered.
D - DisabilityRapid neurologic status using AVPU or Glasgow Coma Scale.Repeat neuro checks often so deterioration is not missed.
E - Exposure/environmentFull-body assessment, hidden injuries, chemical/thermal exposure, hypothermia risk.Remove clothing as needed, preserve evidence, and prevent heat loss.

Thermal and Toxic Emergencies

Rapid support

Cold/heat exposure

  • Frostbite can require rewarming in warm water and tetanus protection.
  • Prevent or treat hypothermia by removing wet clothing, warming the room, using warm blankets, and infusing warmed fluids as prescribed.
  • Heat stroke is an emergency: prioritize ABCDE, oxygen as needed, large-gauge IV access, cooling measures, and urine output monitoring.

Poisoning

  • Identify the agent, provide supportive care, stop absorption, remove the toxin when appropriate, and administer antidotes when ordered.
  • For ingestion, ATI supports activated charcoal, gastric lavage with aspiration when appropriate, and aspiration procedures; ipecac is not recommended.
  • Use naloxone for opioid toxicity and benzodiazepine therapy for seizures when prescribed.

Rapid Response and Cardiac Emergency

Escalate
  • Activate the rapid response team for early signs of sudden decline before arrest or stroke occurs.
  • Expect critical-care support such as an ICU nurse, respiratory therapist, provider, or hospitalist based on facility policy.
  • Use SBAR to communicate and document the event.
  • For ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia, begin BLS components and defibrillate according to protocol.
  • For PEA or asystole, begin BLS components, establish IV access, administer epinephrine per protocol, and consider reversible causes.
PEA reversible causes: think Hs and Ts - hypovolemia, hypoxia, acidosis, potassium imbalance, hypothermia, toxins, tamponade, tension pneumothorax, coronary thrombosis, and pulmonary thrombosis.

Active Learning Scenario

Basic Concept - Five-Level ED Triage

ATI-supported ALS

A nurse in the ED is implementing triage. Complete the Basic Concept template by naming the levels, defining each one, and matching example clients.

LevelDefinitionExample Client
ResuscitationNeeds immediate treatment to prevent death.Cardiac arrest, stroke, pulmonary embolus, overdose.
EmergentTime-sensitive, with risk of life- or limb-threatening progression.Traumatic amputation, head/neck injury, venomous bite.
UrgentNeeds treatment, but not immediately life-threatening.Kidney stone, gallbladder colic, fracture.
Less urgentCan safely wait for a period of time.Bladder infection, laceration, infected toe.
NonurgentRequires simple evaluation or minor care.Rash, minor cut, backache.

Application Exercises

1

On a medical-surgical unit, which client should trigger rapid response team activation?

  • A. Pressure injury with blood glucose of 300 mg/dL.
  • B. New right calf pain with shortness of breath.
  • C. Blood visible on a femoral pressure dressing after cardiac catheterization.
  • D. Dark red toes with absent pedal pulse.
B Calf pain plus shortness of breath can signal pulmonary embolism and rapid respiratory decline.
A, C, D These require assessment and provider notification, but they do not match the same immediate deterioration pattern in this ATI item.
2

A client ingested a toxic agent. Which actions are expected? Select all that apply.

  • A. Induce vomiting.
  • B. Instill activated charcoal as prescribed.
  • C. Prepare for gastric lavage with aspiration when appropriate.
  • D. Administer syrup of ipecac.
  • E. Infuse IV fluids as prescribed.
B, C, E These support toxin binding/removal and circulation.
A, D Vomiting and ipecac increase aspiration risk and are not recommended in this ATI-supported item.
3

An unresponsive client collapsed while pulling weeds. There is no indication of trauma. Which airway technique should the nurse use?

  • A. Head-tilt/chin-lift.
  • B. Modified jaw-thrust.
  • C. Hyperextension of the head.
  • D. Flexion of the head.
A Use head-tilt/chin-lift for an unresponsive client when cervical spine trauma is not suspected.
B Reserve modified jaw-thrust for suspected trauma or cervical spine injury.
4

Which action belongs in the emergency plan for asystole?

  • A. Defibrillate immediately.
  • B. Prepare transcutaneous pacing as the priority.
  • C. Administer IV epinephrine per protocol.
  • D. Elevate the lower extremities as the main intervention.
C ATI supports epinephrine with BLS components, IV access, and evaluation for reversible causes during asystole management.
A Asystole is not a shockable rhythm.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

Basic Concept: five-level triage, ABCDE primary survey, rapid response activation, and toxic/thermal emergency priorities. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after source-supported examples are built.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 3

Neurologic Diagnostic Procedures

Neurologic diagnostic care is about matching the test to the risk: contrast, bleeding, sedation, metal, infection, intracranial pressure, or postprocedure CSF leak. The nurse protects the client before, during, and after the scan or procedure.

TL;DR - Know the Test Risk

For neuro diagnostics, ask: Is contrast used? Is the client pregnant or allergic? Is kidney function adequate? Is bleeding risk present? Is metal or claustrophobia a concern? Does the procedure enter the CNS or dura? Those answers drive the nursing priorities.

High-Yield Safety Checks

  • Contrast procedures: check allergy history and kidney function.
  • Angiography: assess bleeding risk and distal circulation after catheter removal.
  • MRI: remove metal, screen implants, and address claustrophobia.

Priority Follow-Up

  • After lumbar puncture, keep the client flat as prescribed and monitor for headache.
  • For ICP monitoring, preserve system integrity and sterile technique.
  • Use GCS trends to communicate neurologic change quickly.

Diagnostic Procedure Map

Contrast and Imaging Procedures

Pre/Post
ProcedurePurposeNursing Priorities
Cerebral angiographyVisualizes cerebral blood vessels; can identify aneurysm, obstruction, narrowing, tumor vascularity, or guide therapy.NPO as prescribed, assess allergies/bleeding risk/renal function, remove jewelry, monitor puncture site and distal circulation after the procedure.
CT scanCross-sectional cranial images; can identify tumors, infarctions, abnormalities, and guide biopsy needles.If contrast or sedation is used, check NPO status, allergy history, BUN/creatinine, and monitor for reaction or renal changes.
MRIMagnet-based cross-sectional images; useful for soft tissue, tumor size, and blood vessel location without radiation exposure.Remove metal and foil-backed patches, screen for implants, assess claustrophobia, use MRI-approved equipment, and monitor for contrast reaction.
PET/SPECTNuclear medicine images of brain activity or metabolism.Discuss pregnancy risk, assess diabetes management needs, keep the head still during imaging, and monitor for radioisotope reaction when used.
X-rayImages skull/spine structures to identify fractures, curvature, erosion, dislocation, or calcification.Determine pregnancy status, remove jewelry/clothing over the area, and explain that the procedure is quick and requires stillness.

Function, Consciousness, and Pressure

Trend changes

EEG and GCS

  • EEG assesses brain electrical activity and is commonly used for seizure activity, sleep disorders, and behavioral changes.
  • Teach the client to wash hair, avoid oils/gels/sprays, stay awake as instructed, and avoid stimulant or sedative medications if prescribed.
  • GCS reports eye, verbal, and motor response. A total below 8 is associated with severe head injury/coma; 9-12 is moderate; above 13 is minor head trauma.

ICP monitoring

  • Used for early identification and treatment of increased intracranial pressure, especially with coma or GCS 8 or lower.
  • Normal ICP is about 10-15 mm Hg; sustained elevation threatens cerebral circulation.
  • Maintain a closed/sterile system, inspect insertion site, recalibrate per policy, and monitor neurologic status and waveforms.

Lumbar Puncture

CSF care
  • Purpose: withdraw CSF for analysis, reduce CSF pressure, instill contrast/air, or administer medication/chemotherapy into spinal fluid.
  • Preprocedure: discuss risks/benefits, screen for increased ICP or bleeding risk, remove jewelry, have the client void, and position to flex the spine.
  • Postprocedure: monitor puncture site and maintain prescribed bed rest to promote clotting and reduce post-lumbar puncture headache risk.
  • If headache occurs from CSF leakage, support flat positioning, hydration, prescribed pain medication, and possible epidural blood patch if persistent.

Active Learning Scenario

Diagnostic Procedure - MRI With Contrast

ATI-supported ALS

A client is scheduled for an MRI scan with contrast. Complete the Diagnostic Procedure template by defining the test and planning nursing care across the procedure.

Template PartStudy Fill-In
Procedure nameMRI uses magnetic fields to create multiple images of body structures.
PreprocedureRemove jewelry/metal, assess claustrophobia, ask about metal implants, and assess allergies when contrast is planned.
IntraprocedureStabilize the client's head and use MRI-compatible monitoring or oxygen equipment when needed.
PostprocedureMonitor for allergic reaction to contrast media if it was used.

Application Exercises

1

A client reports a throbbing headache when sitting upright after a lumbar puncture. Which actions should the nurse take? Select all that apply.

  • A. Use the Glasgow Coma Scale as the priority response.
  • B. Assist the client to a supine position.
  • C. Administer prescribed opioid medication.
  • D. Encourage increased fluid intake.
  • E. Instruct deep breathing and coughing exercises.
B, C, D Flat positioning, hydration, and prescribed analgesia can relieve post-lumbar puncture headache from CSF leakage.
E Coughing can increase ICP and worsen the headache.
2

A client has a ventriculostomy for ICP monitoring after traumatic head injury. Which complication is directly related to the monitoring device?

  • A. Headache.
  • B. Infection.
  • C. Aphasia.
  • D. Hypertension.
B Infection is a major ventriculostomy risk, so strict asepsis and closed-system care are priorities.
3

A client opens eyes to voice, speaks incoherently/disoriented, and withdraws from pain. Which GCS score should the nurse document?

  • A. E2 + V3 + M5 = 10.
  • B. E3 + V4 + M4 = 11.
  • C. E4 + V5 + M6 = 15.
  • D. E2 + V2 + M4 = 8.
B Eye opening to voice is E3, incoherent/disoriented conversation is V4, and withdrawal from pain is M4.
4

Which client statements should the nurse report before cerebral angiography with contrast? Select all that apply.

  • A. "I think I might be pregnant."
  • B. "I take warfarin."
  • C. "I take antihypertensive medication."
  • D. "I am allergic to shrimp."
  • E. "I ate a light breakfast this morning."
A, B, D, E Pregnancy risk, anticoagulation, shellfish/contrast allergy concern, and food intake before prescribed NPO time all require follow-up.
C Antihypertensive medication alone is not the contraindication highlighted in this ATI item.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

Diagnostic Procedure: MRI with contrast, cerebral angiography, lumbar puncture, ICP monitoring, CT with contrast, or EEG. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after source-supported examples are built.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 4

Pain Management

Pain management begins with believing and reassessing the client's report. The nurse combines pharmacologic and nonpharmacologic strategies, uses functional goals, and watches closely for opioid sedation and respiratory depression.

TL;DR - Pain Is What the Client Says

Pain is subjective, so the client is the best source. Assess location, quality, intensity, timing, setting, associated manifestations, and aggravating/relieving factors. Treat undertreatment as a serious complication, and monitor opioids with both pain and sedation/respiratory assessments.

Pain Types

  • Acute: protective, temporary, resolves with healing.
  • Chronic: ongoing or recurrent, often longer than 3 months.
  • Nociceptive: tissue damage/inflammation; somatic or visceral.
  • Neuropathic: abnormal nerve processing; often burning, shooting, or pins-and-needles.

Safety Priorities

  • Premedicate before painful procedures when appropriate.
  • Only the client should press the PCA button unless a provider authorizes otherwise.
  • Sedation precedes respiratory depression; intervene early.

Pain Management Framework

Focused Pain Assessment

Subjective first
Assessment AreaAsk/Observe
LocationWhere is the pain? Does it radiate? Ask the client to point to the area.
QualitySharp, dull, aching, burning, stabbing, throbbing, shooting, tight, tiring, or unbearable.
MeasureUse a consistent scale to rate current pain, best/worst pain, and response to interventions.
TimingOnset, duration, frequency, constant vs. intermittent pattern.
Setting/functionHow pain affects sleep, work, movement, and ADLs; what the client is doing when pain occurs.
Associated and modifying factorsFatigue, nausea, anxiety, depression, what worsens pain, and what relieves it.
Do not overtrust vital signs: acute pain can temporarily raise pulse, BP, and RR, but physiologic indicators can normalize even when pain persists.

Classifying Pain

Pick therapy
TypeKey FeaturesTypical Management Clue
AcuteProtective, temporary, usually self-limiting, resolves with tissue healing.May show tachycardia, hypertension, anxiety, diaphoresis, or muscle tension.
ChronicNot protective; ongoing or recurrent, often beyond tissue healing or longer than 3 months.May have no vital sign change; can include depression, fatigue, decreased function, or disability.
NociceptiveFrom tissue damage/inflammation outside the CNS/PNS; somatic or visceral.Often throbbing, aching, localized; can respond to nonopioids and opioids.
NeuropathicFrom abnormal or damaged pain nerves.Often shooting, burning, intense, or pins-and-needles; adjuvant medications can help.
BreakthroughAcute flare above the client's usual pain level.Requires additional relief beyond baseline chronic pain control.

Interventions and Medication Safety

Reassess
  • Use both pharmacologic and nonpharmacologic strategies, considering the client's preferences and functional pain goal.
  • Premedicate before painful care such as repositioning, wound care, or invasive diagnostic testing when appropriate.
  • Nonpharmacologic strategies can reduce stress and coping burden: mind-body practices, cognitive approaches, distraction, and other complementary approaches chosen by the client.
  • Nonopioids are appropriate for mild to moderate pain and can be paired with opioids for more intense pain. Track total acetaminophen intake.
  • Opioids are appropriate for moderate to severe pain. Monitor constipation, orthostatic hypotension, urinary retention, nausea/vomiting, sedation, and respiratory depression.
  • PCA provides small, frequent opioid doses with a lockout interval; the client should report inadequate relief rather than waiting until pain is severe.
  • If respiratory rate is below 8/min and shallow or the client is difficult to arouse, stop the opioid, notify/act per protocol, and prepare naloxone as prescribed.

Active Learning Scenario

Basic Concept - Types of Pain

ATI-supported ALS

A group of newly licensed nurses is reviewing pain types on a medical-surgical unit. Complete the Basic Concept template by defining the types and matching characteristics.

TypeDefinition/Characteristics
AcuteProtective and temporary; usually resolves with tissue healing; can produce tachycardia, hypertension, anxiety, diaphoresis, muscle tension, grimacing, guarding, or moaning.
ChronicOngoing or frequently recurring; persists beyond healing or longer than 3 months; can reduce function and contribute to fatigue or depression.
NociceptiveArises from tissue damage or inflammation; can be somatic or visceral and can cause referred pain.
NeuropathicArises from abnormal or damaged nerves; can feel burning, shooting, intense, or like pins-and-needles.

  • D. Neuropathic pain.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 5

Meningitis

Meningitis is inflammation of the meninges around the brain and spinal cord. ATI prioritizes early isolation, rapid infection treatment, neurologic protection, and complication surveillance because bacterial meningitis can progress quickly.

TL;DR - Isolate First

When meningitis is suspected, protect other people first with droplet precautions, then support antibiotics, fever control, low stimulation, and close neurologic monitoring. Watch for increased ICP, SIADH, septic shock, and septic emboli.

Classic Findings

  • Severe constant headache, stiff neck, photophobia, fever, chills, nausea, vomiting, and altered level of consciousness.
  • Kernig and Brudzinski findings can signal meningeal irritation.
  • Meningococcal meningitis can include a red macular rash.

Priority Nursing Moves

  • Initiate droplet precautions as soon as meningitis is suspected.
  • Decrease stimulation and keep the head of bed elevated about 30 degrees.
  • Avoid opioid masking of level-of-consciousness changes when ICP risk is present.

Meningitis Care Map

Types, Transmission, and Prevention

Infection control

Know the organism pattern

  • Viral meningitis is common and often resolves without specific treatment.
  • Fungal meningitis is associated with immunosuppression, including AIDS.
  • Bacterial meningitis is contagious, has high mortality risk, and needs rapid care.

Vaccines ATI highlights

  • Hib vaccination protects infants against one bacterial cause.
  • Pneumococcal vaccination lowers respiratory and CNS infection risk in high-risk adults.
  • Meningococcal vaccine is recommended for adolescents and before communal living, such as college residence halls or military settings.

Assessment and Diagnostics

Recognize fast
  • Risk factors include recent bacterial infection, immunosuppression, invasive procedures, skull fracture or penetrating injury, direct CSF contamination, and crowded living conditions.
  • CSF analysis from lumbar puncture is the definitive diagnostic procedure; bacterial CSF can appear cloudy with elevated WBC/protein, decreased glucose, and increased pressure.
  • CT or MRI can be used to identify increased ICP or abscess.
  • Culture and sensitivity from blood or other body fluids can guide early antimicrobial selection, and CBC can show elevated WBC count.

Nursing Care and Complications

Prevent spread + ICP
PriorityNursing ActionsWhy It Matters
Droplet precautionsPlace in a private room and continue precautions per policy; bacterial cases can require ongoing isolation.Suspected meningitis can spread quickly to close contacts.
Neurologic protectionQuiet room, reduced light, bed rest, head of bed about 30 degrees, seizure precautions, frequent neuro checks.Inflammation can raise ICP and trigger seizures.
Medication supportExpect broad antibiotics until cultures return; use nonopioid fever/headache control and anticonvulsants if seizure/ICP risk is present.Antibiotics treat infection; nonopioids avoid masking level-of-consciousness changes.
Complication surveillanceMonitor for decreased LOC, pupil changes, impaired extraocular movement, dilute blood/concentrated urine, shock, rash, extremity circulation changes, and coagulation changes.Meningitis can progress to increased ICP, SIADH, septic shock, septic emboli, DIC, stroke, gangrene, coma, or death.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 6

Seizures and Epilepsy

Seizures are abrupt abnormal electrical discharges in the brain. Nursing care centers on injury prevention, airway and aspiration protection, accurate event documentation, medication adherence, and rapid treatment of status epilepticus.

TL;DR - Protect, Do Not Restrain

During a seizure, protect privacy and safety, turn the client to the side when possible, loosen restrictive clothing, and never restrain or force the jaw open. Afterward, keep the airway patent, assess, reorient, and document the episode.

Common Triggers

  • Overwhelming fatigue, excessive stress, hyperventilation, flashing lights, excess caffeine, acute alcohol use, and substance exposure.
  • Risk factors include head trauma, infection, stroke, metabolic problems, hypoxia, tumor, and abrupt AED discontinuation.
  • Older adults can develop new seizures related to cerebrovascular or cardiac disease.

Priority Follow-Up

  • Postictal priority is airway/aspiration protection in side-lying position.
  • Teach AEDs at the same time daily and periodic blood levels when prescribed.
  • Status epilepticus is prolonged or repeated seizure activity and requires emergency treatment.

Seizure Care Map

Seizure Patterns

Classify
PatternKey FeaturesStudy Cue
Tonic-clonicBrief stiffening, loss of consciousness, then rhythmic jerking; breathing can pause or become irregular; postictal confusion and sleepiness follow.Big safety and airway event.
Tonic, clonic, myoclonic, atonicTonic = stiffening; clonic = rhythmic jerking; myoclonic = brief jerks or stiffening; atonic = sudden loss of muscle tone with fall risk.Name the movement pattern.
Complex partialFocal seizure with automatisms, possible blackout, and amnesia around the event.Lip smacking or picking at clothes is a clue.
Simple partialFocal seizure with preserved consciousness; can include unusual sensation, deja vu, autonomic changes, unilateral movement, pain, or smell.Awake but altered sensation/motor activity.

During and After a Seizure

Airway + injury

During

  • Provide privacy, ease the client to the floor if standing, move furniture away, loosen clothing, and protect the head.
  • Position to keep the airway patent and turn the client to the side when possible.
  • Do not restrain, force open the jaw, insert an airway during seizure activity, or use padded tongue blades.

After

  • Maintain side-lying position, check vital signs, assess for injury, and perform neurologic checks.
  • Reorient and calm the client, allow rest as needed, and ask about aura or possible triggers.
  • Document onset, duration, LOC, apnea, cyanosis, motor activity, incontinence, and postictal findings.

Medication, Procedures, and Long-Term Safety

Adherence
  • AED therapy often starts with one medication; blood levels help determine therapeutic range, adherence, and effectiveness.
  • Teach clients to take AEDs at the same time every day, report adverse effects, and keep routine dental care if gum overgrowth is a risk.
  • Phenytoin can reduce oral contraceptive effectiveness and has important anticoagulant interactions, so medication review matters.
  • Vagal nerve stimulation can help some partial seizures; the client can hold a magnet over the implanted device at aura onset to abort or lessen a seizure.
  • Clients with vagal nerve stimulators should avoid MRI, ultrasound diathermy, microwave ovens, and shortwave radios per ATI teaching.
  • Encourage medical identification, review state driving rules, and involve school/social/vocational resources when seizures affect access or functioning.

Active Learning Scenario

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 7

Parkinson's Disease

Parkinson's disease is a progressive motor disorder caused by decreased dopamine and relative acetylcholine dominance. Nursing care protects medication timing, swallowing, mobility, communication, cognition, and long-term family support.

TL;DR - Dopamine Down, Acetylcholine Dominates

Parkinson's disease produces tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia, and postural instability. Give medications on time, watch for swallowing and aspiration risk, support mobility while independence remains possible, and reassess cognition and mood as the disease advances.

Expected Findings

  • Pill-rolling tremor, slow shuffling or propulsive gait, stooped posture, and masklike expression.
  • Rigidity, bradykinesia or akinesia, slow monotonous speech, fatigue, and reduced manual dexterity.
  • Difficulty chewing/swallowing, drooling, dysarthria, mood changes, and possible dementia.

Priority Nursing Moves

  • Administer PD medications at prescribed times and monitor for wearing-off periods or dyskinesias.
  • Use swallowing precautions, small frequent meals, thickened/cold fluids when appropriate, and upright positioning.
  • Encourage active ROM, assistive devices, rest periods, and extra time for activities.

Parkinson's Disease Care Map

Pathophysiology and Stages

Motor decline
Stage/CueATI-Supported PatternNursing Meaning
Core problemDegeneration of the substantia nigra lowers dopamine; acetylcholine overstimulates the basal ganglia.Treatment aims to raise dopamine effect or lower acetylcholine effect.
Stage I-IIUnilateral tremor first, then bilateral limb involvement with walking and balance difficulty.Encourage exercise, independence, and support resources early.
Stage IIIMovement slows significantly with gait disturbance and postural instability.Walker or other assistive device can be needed for ambulation.
Stage IV-VRigidity/akinesia disrupt ADLs; later the client can no longer stand or walk and becomes dependent.Focus shifts toward nutrition, aspiration prevention, skin/safety, cognition, and caregiver support.

Mobility, Swallowing, and Communication

Function first

Swallowing and nutrition

  • Consult speech-language therapy for choking risk and dietitian support for semisolid foods or thickened liquids.
  • Track intake, encourage fluids, document weight at least weekly, and offer small frequent meals.
  • Use upright positioning, adaptive eating devices, cold/thick fluids when tolerated, and supplements between meals if weight is falling.

Mobility and communication

  • Encourage active ROM, exercise such as yoga, assistive devices, and planned rest periods.
  • Teach the client to stop occasionally when walking to reduce propulsive gait and fall risk.
  • Encourage slow speech, frequent pauses, facial muscle exercises, alternate communication methods, and speech-language referral.

Medications, Procedures, and Complications

Timing matters
  • Levodopa raises dopamine in the brain; carbidopa can reduce peripheral metabolism so more medication reaches the brain with fewer adverse effects.
  • Dopamine agonists such as bromocriptine, ropinirole, and pramipexole can cause orthostatic hypotension, dyskinesias, and hallucinations.
  • Benztropine and trihexyphenidyl can reduce tremor/rigidity but require monitoring for dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, and acute confusion.
  • Entacapone can make more levodopa available; monitor dyskinesia/hyperkinesia, diarrhea, and expected dark urine.
  • MAO-B inhibitors can reduce wearing-off but require tyramine restriction and caution with sympathomimetics, meperidine, and fluoxetine.
  • Advanced options include stereotactic pallidotomy/thalamotomy and deep brain stimulation; monitor postprocedure for neurologic change, bleeding, infection, or stroke-like findings.
  • Major complications include aspiration pneumonia and altered cognition; use swallowing precautions, suction standby when needed, a safe environment, and interprofessional planning.

Active Learning Scenario

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 8

Alzheimer's Disease

Alzheimer's disease is a nonreversible dementia that progresses over years. Nursing care focuses on safety, structured routines, communication, cognitive support, caregiver education, medication monitoring, and planning for progressive decline.

TL;DR - Safety Plus Structure

Alzheimer's disease progresses from memory lapses to loss of ADLs, continence, swallowing, movement, and environmental awareness. Reduce injury and wandering risk, keep routines predictable, use short directions, and support caregivers before crisis points.

Stage Cues

  • Early: memory lapses, losing items, decreased concentration, difficulty with new names or tasks, but ADLs remain possible.
  • Middle: personal-history gaps, planning/money difficulty, personality or sleep changes, wandering, and possible incontinence.
  • Late: loss of conversation, ADL dependence, swallowing decline, immobility, infection risk, stupor, coma, and death.

Priority Nursing Moves

  • Keep the client away from stairs, exits, elevators, dangerous items, and environmental clutter.
  • Use a structured environment, short directions, consistency, repetition, and therapeutic touch.
  • Use reality orientation in earlier stages and validation therapy in later stages.

Alzheimer's Disease Care Map

Diagnosis, Risk, and Staging

Rule out + stage
  • Dementia involves multiple cognitive deficits that impair memory and can affect language, motor skills, and abstract thinking.
  • There is no single lab test or living diagnostic procedure that definitively diagnoses Alzheimer's disease; testing helps rule out other causes.
  • MRI, CT/CAT, PET, EEG, and sometimes lumbar puncture can support evaluation when ruling out other causes.
  • Risk factors include advanced age, family history, genetic predisposition, previous head injury, female sex, chemical imbalance, and environmental exposures such as herpes virus, metal, or toxic waste.
  • Assessment tools can include MMSE, set test using FACT, Short Blessed Test, or Clock Drawing Test.

Nursing Care: Cognition, Memory, and Behavior

Routine

Cognitive stimulation

  • Offer walks, music, craft activities, and varied but manageable stimulation.
  • Maintain a structured environment and introduce changes gradually.
  • Use calendars, short directions, consistency, repetition, and therapeutic touch.

Memory and behavior

  • Reminisce, use lists/rehearsal, and repeat the client's last statement to stimulate memory.
  • Avoid overstimulation by reducing noise, clutter, and crowds.
  • Reduce agitation with calm redirection, diversions, and toileting schedules.

Safety, Medications, and Family Planning

Caregiver support
NeedNursing/Teaching FocusATI Cue
Home safetyRemove scatter rugs and clutter, improve stair lighting, install handrails, secure dangerous supplies, use hard-to-open locks/door alarms, lower the mattress if needed, and consider Safe Return enrollment.Wandering + falls are central risks.
MedicationDonepezil can slow progression and improve behavior/function for some clients; memantine can reduce deterioration and may be paired with donepezil.These do not cure AD.
MonitoringWatch for frequent stools/upset stomach, dizziness, headache, lightheadedness, unsteady gait, and caution in asthma/COPD.Medication effects can worsen fall or respiratory risk.
Family planningRefer for support groups, social services, case management, adult day care, long-term care options, legal planning, advanced directives, guardianship, and caregiver respite.Care needs intensify over time.

  • D. Previous head injury.
  • E. History of herpes infection.
A, D, E ATI lists metal/toxic exposure, previous head injury, and herpes virus exposure among risk factors.
B, C Estrogen therapy is not taught as a risk factor here, and vitamin E use is not a listed AD risk factor.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

System Disorder: Alzheimer's disease, wandering safety, home safety, cognitive stimulation, memory training, caregiver support, and donepezil/memantine teaching. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after source-supported examples are built.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 9

Brain Tumors

Brain tumors are space-occupying lesions that can interrupt CSF flow, raise intracranial pressure, create focal neurologic deficits, and require precise monitoring before and after biopsy, medication therapy, radiation, or craniotomy.

TL;DR - Space Occupying Means ICP Risk

For brain tumors, think location plus pressure. Supratentorial tumors tend to show headache, visual change, seizures, cognitive/personality change, nausea, and possible paralysis; infratentorial tumors often show cranial nerve, hearing, swallowing, eye movement, balance, and brainstem-related findings.

Assessment Clues

  • General findings can include dysarthria, dysphagia, vertigo, papilledema, hemiparesis, positive Romberg, positive Babinski, and cranial nerve dysfunction.
  • Increasing ICP is a priority pattern: change in level of consciousness, restlessness, unequal pupils, and headache.
  • Benign tumors do not metastasize, but location can still make a tumor life-threatening.

Care Priorities

  • Do not perform lumbar puncture when increasing ICP is suspected because herniation risk rises.
  • Avoid routine opioids when possible because they can reduce level of consciousness and depress respirations.
  • After craniotomy, monitor neurologic status, bleeding, seizure activity, positioning, and straining.

Brain Tumor Care Map

Classification, Location, and Clinical Pattern

Locate first
FrameHigh-Yield TeachingWhy It Matters
OriginPrimary malignant tumors usually arise from neuroglial tissue; secondary malignant tumors are metastatic lesions from cancers elsewhere in the body.Metastatic disease changes goals of care and often makes treatment palliative.
BenignBenign tumors often arise from meninges or cranial nerves, have clearer boundaries, and do not metastasize.They can still cause major harm by compression or cranial nerve impairment.
SupratentorialAbove the tentorium in the cerebral hemispheres; watch for morning headache, visual change, seizures, motor loss, cognition/personality change, nausea, and paralysis.Cerebral hemisphere pressure can present as focal or generalized neurologic change.
InfratentorialBelow the tentorium in the brainstem or cerebellum; watch hearing/ringing changes, facial droop, dysphagia, nystagmus, crossed eyes, ataxia, ANS dysfunction, and cranial nerve findings.Brainstem/cerebellar involvement threatens swallowing, airway, balance, and cranial nerve function.

Diagnostics and Medication Priorities

No LP with ICP
  • Imaging and angiographic studies help define tumor size, location, and spread; biopsy confirms cellular pathology when tissue diagnosis is needed.
  • Lumbar puncture and EEG can provide information, but LP is avoided if the client has manifestations of increasing ICP.
  • Corticosteroids reduce cerebral edema and can improve headache or altered level of consciousness related to swelling.
  • Osmotic diuretics can reduce brain fluid burden and help lower ICP.
  • Anticonvulsants are used to prevent or control seizures; H2-antagonists help reduce stress-ulcer risk during high-stress treatment periods.
  • Antiemetics can be used when nausea or vomiting is present from ICP, tumor location, or treatment.

Craniotomy, Positioning, and Complications

Post-op watch

Before surgery

  • Explain the procedure, answer questions, and include the partner when possible to reduce miscommunication.
  • Review aspirin, anticoagulants, NSAIDs, alcohol, tobacco, and complementary therapy use according to provider instructions.
  • Discuss advance directives and administer prescribed preoperative medications such as anxiolytics or muscle relaxants.

After surgery

  • Monitor vital signs, Glasgow Coma Scale, neurologic status, head dressing drainage, bleeding, and seizures.
  • For supratentorial surgery, keep the head elevated 30 degrees and neutral; avoid neck/hip flexion and straining.
  • For infratentorial surgery, side-lying/flat positioning and frequent turning can be prescribed early after surgery.

Complications to recognize include SIADH, diabetes insipidus, increased ICP, hydrocephalus, postoperative bleeding, and seizures. Report new neurologic decline promptly.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 10

Multiple Sclerosis

Multiple sclerosis is a chronic autoimmune demyelinating disorder of the CNS. Nursing care focuses on recognizing relapse triggers and neurologic findings, preserving mobility and safety, managing bladder/bowel and visual changes, and teaching medication precautions.

TL;DR - Demyelination Causes Disconnected Signals

MS damages myelin in the brain and spinal cord, interrupting nerve impulse transmission. Expect relapses and remissions, fatigue, sensory changes, visual changes, dysarthria, dysphagia, spasticity, ataxia, bowel/bladder dysfunction, cognitive change, and heat-related worsening.

Trigger Watch

  • Relapses can be triggered by infection, stress, fatigue, pregnancy, injury, overexertion, cold-climate association, temperature extremes, and hot showers or baths.
  • Uhthoff's sign is temporary worsening of vision or neurologic function after exertion or heat exposure.
  • Diagnosis often takes time because early findings can be vague.

Care Priorities

  • Monitor vision, speech fatigue, swallowing, activity tolerance, skin integrity, cognition, and coping support.
  • Prevent fatigue and overheating; group care, plan rest, and stretch/exercise involved muscles within tolerance.
  • Teach bladder routines, infection prevention, assistive devices, and referral needs as function changes.

Multiple Sclerosis Care Map

Pathophysiology, Course, and Expected Findings

CNS myelin
  • MS is an autoimmune disorder affecting nerve cells in the brain and spinal cord; demyelination disrupts nerve impulse flow.
  • Plaques can form on damaged axons, causing permanent injury when the axon cannot regenerate.
  • Commonly affected areas include the optic nerve, cerebrum, brainstem, cerebellum, and spinal cord.
  • The most common course is relapsing-remitting; recovery after a relapse might not return the client to the previous baseline.
  • Expected findings include fatigue, pain/paresthesia, diplopia or visual-field changes, tinnitus/vertigo, dysphagia, dysarthria, spasticity, ataxia, nystagmus, bowel/bladder dysfunction, cognitive changes, and sexual dysfunction.

Diagnostics and Nursing Care

Function support

Diagnostics

  • CSF analysis can show elevated protein and a slight increase in WBCs.
  • MRI of the brain and spine is the key diagnostic study because it can show plaques.
  • Assessment must include vision, speech, swallowing, activity tolerance, skin, cognition, bowel, bladder, and mobility.

Nursing care

  • Encourage fluids and bladder routines to lower urinary tract infection risk; timed voiding or intermittent self-catheterization can be used when prescribed.
  • Use eye patches for diplopia and alternate eyes; teach scanning techniques for visual-field problems.
  • Reduce injury risk with a safe environment, wide-base walking, assistive devices, skin precautions, and energy conservation.

Medication Map

Relapse + symptoms
Medication GroupPurposeTeaching / Nursing Watch
Interferon beta and glatiramerDisease-modifying therapy to reduce relapse frequency/duration.Interferon beta can cause flu-like manifestations; injectable therapy is often started early.
Teriflunomide, fingolimod, dimethyl fumarateOral disease-modifying alternatives.Useful when injections are not tolerated or cause reactions.
CorticosteroidsReduce inflammation during acute exacerbations.Monitor for infection risk, fluid/electrolyte changes, hyperglycemia, GI bleeding, and personality change.
Dantrolene, tizanidine, baclofen, diazepamTreat muscle spasticity.Report increased weakness or jaundice; avoid abrupt baclofen discontinuation; monitor liver risk with tizanidine or dantrolene.
Carbamazepine and symptom agentsCarbamazepine for paresthesia; docusate for constipation; propantheline for bladder dysfunction; propranolol/clonazepam for tremor or ataxia.Match medication teaching to the symptom being treated and watch for safety effects such as dizziness or weakness.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 11

Headaches

Headache care starts with distinguishing primary headaches from secondary headaches that need investigation. ATI emphasizes migraine and cluster patterns, trigger recognition, medication timing, comfort measures, and when neurologic imaging is needed.

TL;DR - Migraine Has Triggers, Cluster Has a Clock

Migraine headaches commonly bring unilateral throbbing pain, photophobia, phonophobia, nausea, vomiting, and possible aura. Cluster headaches are brief, intense, unilateral, nonthrobbing attacks that can occur 1 to 8 times daily with tearing, nasal congestion, eyelid changes, and pacing or rocking.

Red Flags

  • Secondary headaches are linked to an organic cause such as tumor or aneurysm and need further investigation.
  • Use neuroimaging for new-onset headaches in a client older than 50 years or when neurologic findings are present.
  • Report changes in headache intensity or new visual or neurologic disturbances.

Priority Moves

  • Maintain a cool, dark, quiet environment and elevate the head of the bed as desired.
  • Teach a headache diary to identify food, environment, hormone, sleep, stress, and medication triggers.
  • For cluster headache, high-flow oxygen at onset can provide rapid relief when prescribed.

Headache Care Map

Primary, Secondary, and Trigger Screening

Classify first
  • Primary headaches have no identifiable organic cause; migraine, tension-like, and cluster headaches fit this group.
  • Secondary headaches are associated with an organic cause and warrant further medical management.
  • Common triggers include alcohol, allergies, intense odors, bright or flickering lights, medication overuse, fatigue, sleep deprivation, depression, anxiety, stress, hormone fluctuations, tyramine, MSG, nitrites, dairy, weather, and altitude changes.
  • Teach stress management, hand hygiene when cold-like symptoms are involved, safe use of over-the-counter medications, and provider review of herbal or supplement remedies.

Migraine Versus Cluster Pattern

Compare
PatternExpected FindingsTeaching Hook
Migraine with auraProdrome can occur hours to days before pain; aura develops over minutes to an hour with neurologic or visual changes; pain can become severe and throbbing with nausea, vomiting, drowsiness, or vertigo.Aura migraines often repeat the same manifestations each time.
Migraine without auraUnilateral pulsating pain aggravated by activity, often with photophobia, phonophobia, nausea, or vomiting, lasting 4 to 72 hr.Rest, low stimulation, and trigger avoidance matter.
Atypical migraineStatus migrainous lasts longer than 72 hr; migrainous infarction includes persistent neurologic manifestations with possible ischemic findings on imaging.Duration and neurologic change raise urgency.
Cluster headacheIntense unilateral nonthrobbing pain lasting 15 min to 3 hr, often daily in cycles, with tearing, runny nose, nasal congestion, facial sweating, eyelid droop or edema, miosis, pallor/flushing, bradycardia, nausea, vomiting, and pacing or rocking.Spring/fall pattern and disrupted sleep-wake rhythm are high-yield cues.

Medication, Oxygen, and Comfort Teaching

Treat early
  • Abortive therapy is used during the aura or soon after headache onset; mild migraines can use NSAIDs, acetaminophen, or migraine-labeled anti-inflammatory combinations as prescribed.
  • Severe migraine therapy can include triptans, ergotamine preparations with caffeine, or other combination agents when prescribed.
  • Preventive therapy can include NSAIDs with beta blockers, calcium channel blockers, beta-adrenergic blockers, antiepileptic medications, or onabotulinumtoxinA for chronic migraine.
  • Teach pulse checks when beta-adrenergic blockers or calcium channel blockers are prescribed.
  • For cluster headache, oxygen at 12 L/min for 15 to 20 min at onset can relieve pain quickly; teach sitting position and home oxygen safety.
  • Complementary strategies such as yoga, meditation, tai chi, exercise, biofeedback, massage, acupuncture, or acupressure can support relaxation and pain management.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 12

Disorders of the Eye

Eye-disorder care protects remaining vision, prevents increased intraocular pressure after procedures, and teaches clients when to report urgent changes. ATI highlights macular degeneration, cataracts, and glaucoma.

TL;DR - Central, Cloudy, Pressure

Macular degeneration causes central vision loss; cataracts cloud the lens with progressive painless vision loss; glaucoma damages the optic nerve through elevated intraocular pressure and can progress to blindness without early diagnosis and treatment.

Pattern Match

  • Macular degeneration: distorted objects, blurred vision, loss of central vision, and lack of depth perception.
  • Cataracts: decreased visual acuity, reduced night vision/color perception, blurred vision, diplopia, visible opacity, and absent red reflex.
  • Open-angle glaucoma: gradual peripheral vision loss, mild eye pain, headache, halos, and IOP greater than 21 mm Hg.

Safety Moves

  • After cataract or glaucoma surgery, avoid bending, straining, coughing, sneezing, tight collars, jerky movement, and other IOP-increasing activities.
  • Teach eye-drop spacing, hand hygiene, avoiding bottle-tip contact, and punctal occlusion when prescribed.
  • Report sudden pain, decreased vision, bleeding/discharge, flashes, floating shapes, nausea/vomiting with eye pain, or infection signs.

Eye Disorder Care Map

Macular Degeneration and Cataracts

Vision pattern
DisorderHigh-Yield FindingsTeaching / Care
Dry macular degenerationMost common AMD type; gradual retinal capillary blockage leads to ischemic macular damage and central vision loss.Increase antioxidant, carotene, vitamin E, and vitamin B12 intake as directed; connect the client with transportation, reading devices, and large-print resources.
Wet macular degenerationLess common; fragile new blood vessels leak blood or fluid.Therapy can include laser treatment or ocular injections such as vascular endothelial growth factor inhibitors.
CataractsLens opacity causes progressive painless vision loss, blurred vision, diplopia, reduced night vision/color perception, visible opacity, and absent red reflex.Support lighting, magnification, large print, talking devices, ophthalmology referral, and post-op infection/IOP teaching.

Glaucoma: Open-Angle Versus Angle-Closure

IOP risk

Open-angle glaucoma

  • Most common form; aqueous humor outflow decreases gradually through drainage-system blockage.
  • Findings include headache, mild eye pain, peripheral vision loss, decreased accommodation, halos around lights, and elevated IOP.
  • Gradual loss of peripheral vision is a key recognition cue.

Angle-closure glaucoma

  • Sudden closure of the iris-sclera angle causes rapid IOP elevation and requires immediate treatment.
  • Findings include blurred/decreased vision, colored halos, nonreactive pupils, severe pain, nausea, and photophobia.
  • IV mannitol can be used in emergency treatment to quickly reduce IOP when prescribed.

Eye Medications and Postoperative Precautions

Teach exact use
  • Atropine ophthalmic solution dilates the pupil for visualization or pre-op care; effects can last 7 to 12 days and photosensitivity requires sunglasses.
  • Glaucoma medications require consistent use; wait 5 to 10 min between drops, avoid touching the bottle tip to the eye, wash hands, and use punctal occlusion after instillation.
  • Miotics such as pilocarpine improve aqueous humor outflow but can blur vision; teach good lighting to reduce fall risk.
  • Timolol is a first-line beta blocker for glaucoma, but systemic absorption can cause bradycardia, hypotension, bronchospasm, and hypoglycemia risk.
  • Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors reduce aqueous humor production and are sulfa-based; ask about sulfa allergy.
  • Prostaglandin analogs increase outflow and can darken the iris over time; do not instill if the cornea is not intact.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 13

Middle and Inner Ear Disorders

Middle and inner ear disorders affect hearing, balance, and fall risk. ATI emphasizes assessment patterns, otoscopy and vestibular testing, vertigo medication safety, middle-ear surgery restrictions, and cochlear implant teaching.

TL;DR - Hearing Plus Balance Equals Fall Risk

Middle-ear problems often look like ear pain, fullness, conductive hearing loss, fever, bulging or inflamed tympanic membrane, and altered light reflex. Inner-ear problems often add vertigo, tinnitus, nausea/vomiting, nystagmus, altered balance, and sensorineural hearing changes.

Differentiate

  • Conductive hearing loss can result from otitis media, otosclerosis, or foreign body/impacted cerumen.
  • Sensorineural hearing loss involves cranial nerve VIII damage and can be associated with aging or ototoxic medication exposure.
  • BPPV is position-triggered vertigo; Meniere disease adds episodic vertigo, tinnitus, and fluctuating sensorineural hearing loss.

Safety Moves

  • Use fall precautions, assistive devices, slow position changes, and a clutter-free environment for vertigo or balance limits.
  • Teach sedation precautions with meclizine, diphenhydramine, dimenhydrinate, scopolamine, or diazepam.
  • After middle-ear surgery, avoid air travel, straining/coughing, forceful nose blowing, water in the ear, and rapid movement until cleared.

Ear Disorder Care Map

Hearing Loss, Otitis Media, and Vertigo Patterns

Locate the problem
PatternExpected FindingsPriority Teaching
Otitis media / middle earEar pain or pressure, fever, headache, conductive hearing loss, red/bulging TM, altered light reflex, and purulent or bloody drainage if perforation occurs.Antibiotics, analgesics, heat, decongestants, or myringotomy/pressure-equalizing tube can be used as prescribed.
BPPVSudden vertigo with position change, related to disrupted canal debris.Move slowly, restrict head movement during episodes, and use short-course antivertigo medication when prescribed.
Meniere diseaseEpisodic vertigo, tinnitus, and fluctuating sensorineural hearing loss.Evenly space fluids, reduce sodium/MSG, avoid caffeine and alcohol, and use assistive devices for balance safety.
LabyrinthitisSudden severe vertigo, nausea, vomiting, possible hearing loss, and tinnitus, often secondary to otitis media.Rest in a darkened environment; meclizine or dimenhydrinate can treat vertigo/nausea as prescribed.

Diagnostics and Medication Safety

Sedation watch
  • Audiometry checks hearing ability by frequency, pitch, and intensity.
  • Tympanogram measures tympanic membrane and middle-ear mobility and can help diagnose middle-ear disease.
  • Weber and Rinne tuning-fork tests help determine whether hearing loss is present.
  • Otoscopy should show a pearly gray intact TM, visible malleus, and normal light reflex; fluid or bubbles behind the TM are unexpected.
  • ENG and caloric testing assess vestibular function through eye-movement responses; the client is kept on bed rest and NPO until vertigo subsides after the procedure.
  • Monitor ototoxic medication exposure, including gentamicin, erythromycin, furosemide, ethacrynic acid, aspirin, ibuprofen, and cisplatin.
  • Meclizine, antihistamines, scopolamine, and diazepam can help vertigo/nausea but require sedation, urinary retention, fall, glaucoma, and older-adult safety monitoring.

Middle-Ear Surgery and Cochlear Implant Teaching

Protect the ear

After middle-ear surgery

  • Avoid air travel for 2 to 3 weeks.
  • Avoid straining/coughing and blow the nose gently with the mouth open for 2 to 3 weeks.
  • Keep the ear canal clean and dry; avoid washing hair or showering for several days to 1 week.
  • Expect temporary hearing loss from fluid or packing; report drainage from the ear canal.

Cochlear implant

  • Used for sensorineural hearing loss.
  • Includes microphone, speech processor, transmitter/receiver, and electrodes attached to the auditory nerve.
  • The unit is not turned on immediately after surgery; the external unit and processor programming happen later.
  • Teach infection prevention and MRI avoidance.

Active Learning Scenario

IndicationSensorineural hearing loss.
Nursing interventionsComplete pre- and postoperative teaching, provide outpatient surgical care, teach that the unit is not turned on immediately, teach that the external unit and speech processor are programmed later, prevent infection, and avoid MRI.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 14

Head Injury

Head injury care is a neurologic emergency. ATI priorities are cervical spine protection, oxygenation, early neurologic deterioration recognition, ICP reduction, seizure/safety precautions, and family support.

TL;DR - Stabilize C-Spine, Protect Oxygen, Watch ICP

Assume cervical spine injury with head trauma until cleared. Respiratory status is the priority assessment, changes in level of consciousness are the earliest sign of neurologic decline, and ICP rises with hypercarbia, suctioning, coughing, Valsalva, neck/hip flexion, low HOB, and stimulation.

Injury Patterns

  • Concussion changes neurologic function without identified brain damage and usually resolves within 72 hr.
  • Contusion bruises the brain and involves unconsciousness with stupor or confusion.
  • Diffuse axonal injury causes widespread injury and coma after severe trauma; hemorrhage can occur epidural, subdural, or intracerebral.

Emergency Cues

  • Test bloody drainage for a halo sign if CSF leak from nose or ears is suspected.
  • Increased ICP findings include severe headache, nausea/vomiting, declining LOC, restlessness, nonreactive pupils, abnormal breathing, posturing, Cushing triad, and seizures.
  • Brain herniation signs include fixed dilated pupils, deteriorating LOC, Cheyne-Stokes respirations, hemodynamic instability, and abnormal posturing.

Head Injury Care Map

Assessment Priorities and Diagnostics

ABC + GCS
  • Maintain cervical spine stability until imaging clears the neck.
  • Assess respiratory status first because brain tissue has minimal reserve when oxygen is deprived.
  • Use the Glasgow Coma Scale and level-of-consciousness trends to detect early neurologic deterioration.
  • Assess pupils, cranial nerve function, bilateral sensory/motor responses, seizure activity, and evidence of CSF leakage.
  • Diagnostics can include cervical spine films, CT/MRI of head or neck, labs for oxygenation/electrolytes/osmolality/glucose/toxicology, and cerebral perfusion calculation when ICP monitoring is in place.

Increased ICP: Prevent the Rise

Reduce stimulation

Raises ICP

  • Hypercarbia, suctioning, coughing, extreme neck or hip flexion/extension, HOB below 30 degrees, restrictive clothing, and Valsalva.
  • Overhydration, noisy environments, frequent stimulation, and uncontrolled pain/restlessness can worsen risk.
  • Opioids can obscure neurologic assessment and depress respirations if the client is not mechanically ventilated.

Lowers ICP risk

  • Elevate HOB at least 30 degrees and keep head/body midline and neutral.
  • Maintain airway, give oxygen as indicated, limit noise/visitors, and use stool softeners to avoid Valsalva.
  • Monitor fluids, electrolytes, osmolality, strict intake/output, daily weight, and signs of DI or SIADH.

Medications, Craniotomy, and Complications

Rapid response
  • Mannitol treats cerebral edema by drawing fluid from brain tissue into the blood; monitor urine output, renal status, electrolytes, osmolality, hypovolemia, and hyponatremia.
  • Barbiturate coma can decrease cellular metabolic demand but requires mechanical ventilation, cardiac/hemodynamic monitoring, and ICP monitoring.
  • Phenytoin can prevent or treat seizures; monitor therapeutic blood levels and interactions.
  • Craniotomy can remove nonviable tissue, evacuate epidural/subdural hematomas, reduce ICP, or remove tumors.
  • Brain herniation, hematoma/hemorrhage, pulmonary edema, DI, and SIADH are major complications requiring close neurologic and physiologic monitoring.

Active Learning Scenario

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 15

Stroke

Stroke care moves quickly: identify whether the event is ischemic or hemorrhagic, protect oxygenation and cerebral perfusion, screen swallowing before intake, and build safety around communication, vision, neglect, and mobility changes.

TL;DR - Type, Time, Swallow, Side

First priority is rapid recognition and imaging so treatment matches the stroke type. Ischemic stroke can qualify for thrombolytic therapy only within the ordered time window and only after bleeding is ruled out. After stabilization, protect from aspiration, injury, immobility complications, and frustration from language or perception changes.

Classify Fast

  • Hemorrhagic stroke involves vessel rupture and rising intracranial pressure risk from bleeding.
  • Thrombotic ischemic stroke often evolves as a clot forms on plaque.
  • Embolic ischemic stroke can begin suddenly when material from another site blocks cerebral flow.

Protect Function

  • Keep oxygen saturation above the ordered goal and monitor neurologic status closely.
  • Screen gag and swallowing before oral intake; keep suction available when feeding begins.
  • Teach scanning, positioning, and affected-side safety for hemianopsia or unilateral neglect.

Stroke Care Map

Stroke Type, Side, and TIA Warning

Localization

Patterns

  • Transient ischemic attack can present with brief visual changes, dizziness, slurred speech, or extremity weakness and can warn of an impending stroke.
  • Left-hemisphere stroke commonly affects language, reading, writing, calculation, recognition, and the right side of the body.
  • Right-hemisphere stroke commonly affects visual-spatial awareness, depth perception, impulse control, judgment, and the left side of the body.

Risk Reduction

  • Control hypertension, blood glucose, lipids, weight, tobacco exposure, and activity level.
  • Recognize high-risk conditions such as atrial fibrillation, atherosclerosis, hypercoagulability, aneurysm, and arteriovenous malformation.
  • Use prescribed antithrombotic therapy or carotid intervention when indicated for prevention.

Rapid Diagnostics and Acute Priorities

ED window
  • Noncontrast CT is the rapid first-line test to help distinguish ischemic from hemorrhagic stroke and determine thrombolytic eligibility.
  • MRI, MRA, cerebral angiography, and lumbar puncture can help evaluate ischemia, edema, aneurysm, arteriovenous malformation, perfusion, or blood in cerebrospinal fluid when ordered.
  • Monitor vital signs, temperature, oxygenation, cardiac rhythm, glucose, and level of consciousness because fever, hypoxia, dysrhythmias, hyperglycemia, and increased ICP worsen risk.
  • Position with HOB about 30 degrees, head midline, and neck neutral; avoid flexion or extension that can impair venous drainage.
  • Alteplase or other thrombolytic therapy is time-sensitive for eligible ischemic stroke and is contraindicated if hemorrhage or major bleeding risk is present.

Dysphagia, Neglect, and Rehab Safety

Aspiration + injury prevention

Swallow and Communication

  • Assess comprehension with simple commands and verify yes/no accuracy before relying on responses.
  • Use slow, clear speech, one-step instructions, picture boards, and extra response time for aphasia.
  • Keep the client NPO until swallowing safety is evaluated when dysphagia is suspected.
  • Use prescribed food texture and fluid thickness, feed on the unaffected side, and keep the head/neck slightly flexed forward when swallowing.

Mobility and Vision

  • Protect the affected arm and shoulder; support the limb in bed, wheelchair, and during ambulation to reduce subluxation risk.
  • Perform active ROM on the unaffected side and passive ROM on the affected side; elevate swollen extremities and move edema toward the body as taught.
  • Teach scanning toward the affected visual field for homonymous hemianopsia.
  • For unilateral neglect, teach dressing the affected side first and checking the affected limb for injury.

Unit 2 · Neurologic Disorders · Chapter 16

Spinal Cord Injury

Spinal cord injury care protects the cord first, then prevents secondary injury from hypoxia, hypotension, shock, immobility, bladder/bowel complications, skin breakdown, and autonomic dysreflexia.

TL;DR - Immobilize, Breathe, Perfuse, Decompress Triggers

In acute SCI, prevent further cord damage by minimizing movement and supporting oxygenation/perfusion. C4 or higher threatens spontaneous ventilation. Later care focuses on bowel/bladder routines, skin protection, gradual mobility, spasticity management, and rapid response to autonomic dysreflexia.

Level Predicts Loss

  • Cervical injury can cause quadriplegia and respiratory compromise.
  • Injury below T1 can cause paraplegia; upper thoracic lesions can also impair trunk stability.
  • Complete injury causes loss of voluntary movement and sensation below the lesion; incomplete injury causes variable loss.

Shock and Dysreflexia

  • Spinal shock causes temporary flaccidity, absent reflexes, and autonomic loss below the injury.
  • Neurogenic shock produces hypotension, bradycardia, dependent edema, and temperature dysregulation.
  • Autonomic dysreflexia is an emergency: sit upright, find the trigger, and treat severe hypertension.

Spinal Cord Injury Care Map

Assessment, Imaging, and Respiratory Priority

ABC + cord level
  • Assess motor function, sensation, reflexes, bowel/bladder control, pain, respiratory depth, and blood pressure response to position changes.
  • Use light touch, sharp/dull discrimination, and hot/cold discrimination to map sensory loss below the lesion.
  • CT, MRI, and x-rays help identify cord injury extent, fractures, blood, or bone fragments.
  • Respiratory status is the first priority because high cervical or upper thoracic lesions can impair involuntary or voluntary breathing.
  • Provide oxygen, suction, intubation or ventilation support as needed; assist coughing with abdominal pressure and teach incentive spirometry when appropriate.

Shock, Immobilization, and Mobility

Prevent secondary injury

Perfusion and Tone

  • Monitor for neurogenic shock within the first 24 hr and for several days to weeks: hypotension, bradycardia, edema, and poor temperature regulation.
  • Support ordered mean arterial pressure goals and treat adverse findings with prescribed fluids, vasopressors, or atropine.
  • Upper motor neuron injuries can become spastic after neurogenic shock resolves; lower motor neuron injuries often remain flaccid.
  • Use anticoagulation prophylaxis and assess for VTE findings because immobility and venous pooling increase risk.

Stabilization

  • Maintain alignment and immobilization until traction or surgery stabilizes the spine.
  • For halo fixation or cervical tongs, keep weights hanging freely, monitor skin and pin sites, and never use the halo device to turn or move the client.
  • After anterior cervical fusion, watch for airway compromise from swelling or bleeding and assess for tracheal deviation.
  • Transfer gradually to prevent postural hypotension, using a reclining wheelchair and lowering the angle if dizziness occurs.

Bowel, Bladder, Skin, and Autonomic Dysreflexia

Long-term safety
  • Turn at least every 2 hr in bed and every 1 hr in a wheelchair; use pressure-relief surfaces because the client might not feel prolonged pressure.
  • Spastic bladder after upper motor neuron injury can be managed with male condom catheter options or indwelling catheterization for female clients when urine release is unpredictable.
  • Flaccid bladder after lower motor neuron injury can require intermittent catheterization or manual bladder expression.
  • Maintain a bowel schedule with stool softeners or bulk-forming laxatives; use suppository or digital stimulation only as ordered and cautiously because vagal response can cause bradycardia or syncope.
  • Autonomic dysreflexia can cause severe hypertension, headache, sweating, pallor below the lesion, flushing above the lesion, blurred vision, bradycardia, nausea, and nasal stuffiness.
  • For dysreflexia, sit the client upright, notify the provider, check for bladder distention or catheter obstruction, remove fecal impaction as ordered, loosen tight clothing, control drafts, assess for injury or infection, monitor vital signs, and give prescribed antihypertensives.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 17

Respiratory Diagnostic Procedures

Respiratory diagnostics help answer three urgent questions: how well oxygen and carbon dioxide are moving, whether the airway can be visualized or sampled safely, and whether pleural fluid or air is compressing lung tissue.

TL;DR - Test the Air, Guard the Airway

PFTs measure lung function, ABGs measure oxygenation and acid-base status, bronchoscopy requires NPO and postprocedure gag-return safety, and thoracentesis removes or samples pleural fluid or air while the nurse watches for pneumothorax, bleeding, infection, and respiratory decline.

Before the Test

  • For PFTs, avoid smoking for 6 to 8 hr before testing and follow facility directions for holding inhalers.
  • For ABGs, verify radial and ulnar circulation with Allen's test when radial puncture is planned.
  • For bronchoscopy or thoracentesis, verify consent and procedure-specific preparation.

After the Test

  • After ABG puncture, hold pressure long enough to stop bleeding and monitor circulation at the site.
  • After bronchoscopy, do not resume oral intake until gag reflex and swallowing are safe.
  • After thoracentesis, monitor breath sounds, oxygenation, dressing drainage, and postprocedure chest x-ray results.

Respiratory Diagnostic Care Map

PFTs and ABGs: Function, Oxygenation, Acid-Base

Measurement

PFTs

  • Used to evaluate lung volumes, capacities, diffusion, gas exchange, flow rates, airway resistance, and ventilation distribution.
  • Helpful for dyspnea evaluation, lung disease identification, and preoperative respiratory risk screening.
  • Teach the client not to smoke 6 to 8 hr before testing and to follow instructions about holding inhalers before the test.

ABGs

  • Measure pH, PaO2, PaCO2, HCO3, and SaO2 to evaluate oxygenation and acid-base balance.
  • Before radial arterial puncture, perform Allen's test to confirm collateral circulation.
  • Collect in a heparinized syringe, send the specimen promptly, hold pressure at least 5 min after puncture, and hold at least 20 min if the client receives anticoagulants.
  • Monitor for bleeding, swelling, pulse loss, color change, temperature change, hematoma, arterial occlusion, or air embolism findings.

Bronchoscopy: Visualize, Biopsy, Clear

Airway safety
  • Bronchoscopy visualizes the larynx, trachea, and bronchi; it can identify abnormalities, obtain biopsy or cultures, remove secretions or foreign bodies, and treat selected airway problems.
  • Preprocedure priorities include consent, allergy and anticoagulant review, denture removal, NPO status for aspiration prevention, and ordered premedications.
  • During the procedure, monitor vital signs, respiratory pattern, and oxygenation; older adult clients with respiratory insufficiency are at increased sedation risk.
  • Afterward, monitor respirations, blood pressure, pulse oximetry, heart rate, level of consciousness, cough/gag return, swallowing ability, fever, cough, hemoptysis, hypoxemia, aspiration, and laryngospasm.
  • Expected mild findings can include a sore throat, dry cough, mild fever for less than 24 hr, and small blood-tinged sputum; bronchospasm or significant hemoptysis is not expected.

Thoracentesis: Pleural Fluid or Air Removal

Stillness + follow-up
  • Thoracentesis punctures the chest wall and pleural space to obtain specimens, instill medication, or remove pleural fluid or air.
  • Use imaging or assessment findings to locate the effusion; position the client upright with arms and shoulders supported and feet stable.
  • Teach the client to remain still and avoid coughing or talking unless instructed because movement increases needle injury risk.
  • During the procedure, use strict surgical asepsis, monitor vital signs, skin color, and oxygenation, measure fluid removed, and send specimens promptly.
  • Fluid removal is limited to 1 L at a time to reduce re-expansion pulmonary edema risk.
  • Afterward, apply and assess the dressing, monitor respiratory status hourly for the first several hours, auscultate for reduced breath sounds, encourage deep breathing, and obtain the postprocedure chest x-ray.

Active Learning Scenario

ATI-supported ALS

Template PartStudy Fill-In

Which supplies should be available for thoracentesis? Select all that apply.

  • A. Oxygen equipment.
  • B. Incentive spirometer.
  • C. Pulse oximeter.
  • D. Sterile dressing.
  • E. Suture removal kit.
A, C, D Oxygen, pulse oximetry, and sterile dressing supplies support monitoring and postpuncture care.
B, E Incentive spirometry is more specific to postoperative pulmonary expansion, and suture removal is not part of thoracentesis setup.
5

After thoracentesis, which findings indicate possible complications? Select all that apply.

  • A. Dyspnea.
  • B. Localized bloody drainage contained on the dressing.
  • C. Fever.
  • D. Hypotension.
  • E. Pain at the puncture site.
A, C, D Dyspnea can signal pneumothorax or reaccumulated fluid, fever can indicate infection, and hypotension can indicate intrathoracic bleeding.
B, E Localized contained bloody drainage and puncture-site pain can be expected findings after the procedure.
