Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 18

Chest Tube Insertion and Monitoring

Chest tube care keeps negative pressure working so air, blood, fluid, or pus can leave the pleural space and the lung can re-expand. The nurse protects the water seal, drainage path, insertion site, and emergency supplies.

TL;DR - Keep It Low, Upright, Sealed

Keep the drainage system upright and below chest level, maintain the prescribed water seal and suction, watch tidaling and bubbling patterns, never clamp/strip/milk unless specifically prescribed, and keep sterile water, enclosed hemostats, and an occlusive dressing at the bedside.

Expected System

  • First chamber collects drainage.
  • Second chamber is the water seal that lets air out and prevents air from entering.
  • Third chamber controls suction, either wet or dry depending on the system.

Red Flags

  • Continuous bubbling in the water seal chamber suggests an air leak.
  • No tidaling can mean lung re-expansion or system obstruction.
  • SaO2 below 90%, visible chest tube eyelets, unexpected drainage change, or complications require immediate provider notification.

Chest Tube Care Map

Drainage System Function

Three chambers

What It Does

  • Chest tubes drain fluid, blood, or air; restore negative pressure; support lung expansion; and reestablish normal intrapleural pressure.
  • Indications include pneumothorax, hemothorax, postoperative chest drainage, pleural effusion, and pulmonary empyema.
  • Air tubes are placed toward the apex; fluid drainage tubes are placed closer to the lung base.

What to Watch

  • Keep the system below chest level and upright, including during ambulation.
  • Maintain the water seal level and prescribed wet or dry suction setting.
  • Expect tidaling in the water seal chamber; continuous bubbling belongs in the suction chamber, not the water seal chamber.
  • With mediastinal tubes after open heart surgery, bubbling and tidaling are not expected, though pulsations can be seen.

Preprocedure Through Postprocedure Priorities

Setup + monitoring
  • Before insertion, verify consent, assess anesthetic allergies, position the client as ordered, prepare the drainage system, give prescribed pain/sedation medications, and prep the insertion site.
  • During insertion, assist with dressing and drainage-system setup, keep tubing straight from bed to system, and place the drainage system below chest level.
  • After insertion, assess vital signs, breath sounds, SaO2, color, and respiratory effort at least every 4 hr and more often if unstable.
  • Encourage coughing and deep breathing every 2 hr, position in semi- to high-Fowler's, and administer prescribed pain medication.
  • Document drainage amount and color hourly for the first 24 hr, then at least every 8 hr; report excessive drainage, cloudy drainage, red drainage, stopped early drainage, or drainage above the ordered amount.
  • Keep two enclosed hemostats, sterile water, and an occlusive dressing at the bedside.

Air Leaks, Dislodgement, Tension Pneumothorax, and Removal

Emergency readiness
  • Continuous bubbling in the water seal chamber indicates an air leak; check connections, notify the provider, and use a padded clamp only if prescribed and only briefly to locate the leak.
  • If tubing separates, have the client exhale and cough; if the drainage system is compromised, place the chest tube end in sterile water to create a temporary seal.
  • If the chest tube is accidentally removed, apply dry sterile gauze to the site first, then secure as directed and assess respiratory status.
  • Suspect tension pneumothorax with tracheal deviation, absent unilateral breath sounds, distended neck veins, respiratory distress, chest asymmetry, or cyanosis; notify the provider or rapid response team immediately.
  • Before removal, give pain medication 30 min prior; during removal, coach Valsalva or breath-holding as directed, then apply an airtight sterile petroleum gauze dressing and monitor for recurrence or infection.

Active Learning Scenario

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 19

Respiratory Management and Mechanical Ventilation

Respiratory support starts with the least oxygen needed to correct hypoxemia, then escalates to positive-pressure support or mechanical ventilation when the client cannot maintain a patent airway, oxygenation, or ventilation.

TL;DR - Lowest Oxygen, Secure Airway, Respect Alarms

Choose the oxygen device that matches the client's needed FiO2, titrate to the prescribed saturation, prevent oxygen-related complications, verify every artificial airway, and treat ventilator alarms as client-safety signals until the cause is found.

Oxygen Device Logic

  • Nasal cannula is easy to tolerate but delivers variable oxygen and can dry nares.
  • Simple masks need at least 5 L/min to reduce CO2 rebreathing risk.
  • Venturi masks deliver the most precise FiO2 and are useful when exact oxygen control matters.

Ventilator Priorities

  • Document tube placement, use two staff for repositioning, and keep a manual resuscitation bag at the bedside.
  • Never silence or ignore ventilator alarms without assessing the client and system.
  • Watch weaning for tachypnea, desaturation, dysrhythmias, anxiety, diaphoresis, and declining tidal volume.

Respiratory Support Care Map

Oxygen Delivery Devices: Match Device to Need

FiO2 selection

Low-flow systems

  • Nasal cannula is safe, comfortable, and allows eating and talking, but FiO2 varies with flow and breathing pattern.
  • Assess nares, fit prongs correctly, use water-soluble gel for dryness, and add humidification for higher flows per facility practice.
  • Simple face masks cover the nose and mouth; keep flow at least 5 L/min to help flush CO2 from the mask.
  • Partial rebreather masks require the reservoir bag to stay inflated during inspiration.
  • Nonrebreather masks deliver very high oxygen concentrations; keep the bag partly inflated and check the valve and flaps frequently.

High-flow and humidified systems

  • Venturi masks deliver precise oxygen concentrations through adapters and are well suited for clients who need exact FiO2 control.
  • Check the ordered adapter/flow, keep tubing free of kinks, and assess skin under the mask.
  • Aerosol masks, face tents, tracheostomy collars, and T-pieces provide high humidity and require frequent equipment checks.
  • For humidified devices, drain condensation, keep water in the humidification canister, verify mist during inspiration and expiration, and avoid pulling on the airway.

Oxygen Therapy: Titrate, Monitor, Prevent Harm

Safety

Oxygenation assessment

  • Early hypoxemia can show as tachypnea, tachycardia, restlessness, pallor, elevated blood pressure, accessory muscle use, nasal flaring, tracheal tugging, or adventitious sounds.
  • Late hypoxemia can progress to confusion, stupor, cyanosis, bradypnea, bradycardia, hypotension, and dysrhythmias.
  • Use semi-Fowler's or Fowler's position, monitor respiratory rate, effort, lung sounds, SaO2, and ABGs, and titrate oxygen to the prescribed saturation.

Complication prevention

  • Use the lowest oxygen level that corrects hypoxemia and monitor for oxygen toxicity when high concentrations or prolonged therapy are needed.
  • In clients with COPD and chronic hypercarbia, monitor level of consciousness, respiratory pattern, and SaO2 for oxygen-induced hypoventilation.
  • Post oxygen-in-use signs, keep flammable materials away, use cotton gowns when indicated, and ensure electrical equipment is grounded.
  • Promote oral care, turning, coughing, deep breathing, incentive spirometry, suctioning, rest, nutrition, hydration, and skin protection.

Positive-Pressure Support and Mechanical Ventilation

Airway support

Escalation path

  • CPAP keeps airways open throughout the respiratory cycle and improves gas exchange during spontaneous breathing.
  • BiPAP delivers a higher inspiratory pressure and a lower expiratory pressure, often for clients with COPD who need ventilatory assistance.
  • Endotracheal intubation provides emergency airway management; confirm placement with end-tidal carbon dioxide and chest x-ray, then auscultate bilateral breath sounds and watch chest movement.
  • Keep resuscitation equipment, including a manual bag and face mask, at the bedside.

Ventilator care

  • Mechanical ventilation supports gas exchange, decreases work of breathing, and can be delivered through an ET or tracheostomy tube.
  • Document tube placement in centimeters at the teeth or lips, use two staff members when repositioning or resecuring, and support tubing to prevent displacement.
  • Suction oral and tracheal secretions to maintain patency, assess respiratory status every 1 to 2 hr, and monitor/document ventilator settings hourly.
  • Low-pressure alarms suggest disconnection, cuff leak, or displacement; high-pressure alarms suggest secretions, biting, kinks, coughing, pulmonary edema, bronchospasm, or pneumothorax.
  • Assess cuff pressure at least every 8 hr and monitor for signs of air leak, inadequate oxygenation, or accidental extubation risk.

Modes, Adjuncts, Weaning, and Complications

Ventilator judgment

Modes and adjuncts

  • Assist-control delivers preset rate and tidal volume and can require sedation if the client hyperventilates.
  • SIMV delivers synchronized machine breaths while allowing spontaneous breaths; it can be used for weaning but can increase work of breathing.
  • PEEP improves oxygenation by keeping alveoli open at end expiration and helping prevent atelectasis.
  • PSV decreases the work of spontaneous breathing by delivering preset pressure during inspiration; CPAP supplies positive pressure during spontaneous breathing.

Weaning and complications

  • Weaning intolerance includes respirations greater than 30/min or less than 8/min, blood pressure or heart rate changes more than 20%, SaO2 less than 90%, dysrhythmias, decreasing tidal volume, labored breathing, diaphoresis, anxiety, or decreased level of consciousness.
  • After extubation, monitor for ineffective cough, dyspnea, stridor, airway obstruction, and respiratory distress; assess SpO2 and vital signs every 5 min initially.
  • Mechanical ventilation can cause barotrauma, volutrauma, fluid retention, oxygen toxicity, decreased venous return, aspiration, and stress ulcers.
  • Keep the head of bed elevated 30 degrees for clients receiving enteral feedings unless contraindicated, and monitor residuals per protocol.

Active Learning Scenario

Therapeutic Procedure - Mechanical Ventilation

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is planning care for a client who is receiving mechanical ventilation through an endotracheal tube.

Maintain the airway

  • Assess tube position and placement, and document placement in centimeters at the teeth or lips.
  • Use two staff members when repositioning or resecuring the tube.
  • Use caution during movement and apply protective barriers according to policy when self-extubation risk is present.

Prevent loss of ventilation

  • Suction oral and tracheal secretions to maintain tube patency.
  • Support ventilator tubing to prevent mucosal erosion or tube displacement.
  • Keep a manual resuscitation bag with a face mask at the bedside for ventilator malfunction or accidental extubation.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 20

Acute Respiratory Disorders

Acute respiratory disorders range from rhinitis and sinusitis to influenza and pneumonia, but the nursing priority stays steady: maintain airway patency, promote oxygenation, reduce transmission, and catch deterioration early.

TL;DR - Airway First, Infection Control Always

For acute respiratory disorders, prioritize airway clearance, oxygenation, hydration, rest, hand hygiene, cough etiquette, immunization, and cultures before antibiotics when pneumonia is suspected.

Upper Airway

  • Rhinitis causes rhinorrhea, congestion, sneezing, pruritus, watery eyes, sore throat, inflamed mucosa, and low-grade fever.
  • Sinusitis causes congestion, headache, facial pressure worse when leaning forward, cough, purulent or bloody drainage, and orbital/facial tenderness.
  • Decongestants can cause rebound congestion if used longer than 3 to 4 days.

Influenza and Pneumonia

  • Influenza prevention leans on yearly vaccination, hand hygiene, cough etiquette, and avoiding close contact during outbreaks.
  • Pneumonia in older adults can present vaguely, with confusion from hypoxia as a common manifestation.
  • For pneumonia, oxygen comes first when saturation is low, then sputum culture before antibiotics when possible.

Acute Respiratory Disorder Care Map

Risk, Rhinitis, and Sinusitis

Upper airway

Prevention and risk

  • Use hand hygiene, respiratory etiquette, vaccination, allergen reduction, and smoking cessation to reduce acute respiratory disorders.
  • Risk rises with very young or older age, lack of current influenza or pneumococcal immunization, tobacco smoke, substance use, chronic lung disease, immunocompromise, aspiration risk, immobility, impaired secretion clearance, and mechanical ventilation.
  • Rhinitis can be infectious or allergic and often overlaps with asthma or other allergy patterns.

Rhinitis and sinusitis care

  • For rhinitis, encourage 8 to 10 hr of rest, at least 2,000 mL/day of fluid if not contraindicated, humidification or steam, tissue disposal, and cough etiquette.
  • Antihistamines, leukotriene inhibitors, mast cell stabilizers, decongestants, intranasal glucocorticoids, antipyretics, and antibiotics can be used depending on cause and symptoms.
  • For sinusitis, encourage steam, sinus irrigation, saline sprays, hot wet packs, fluids, rest, and correct nasal spray or irrigation technique.
  • Report severe headache, nuchal rigidity, or high fever because meningitis or encephalitis can occur if pathogens spread.

Influenza: Contagious Window and Prevention

Viral illness

Recognize

  • Seasonal influenza is highly contagious and commonly occurs in fall and winter.
  • Adults can transmit influenza from 24 hr before symptoms begin until about 5 days after onset.
  • Expected findings include severe headache, muscle aches, chills, fatigue, weakness, fever, and cough; severe diarrhea, cough, and hypoxia are emphasized for avian influenza patterns.

Act

  • Use droplet and contact precautions for hospitalized clients with pandemic influenza.
  • Monitor respiratory status, hydration, intake, and output; provide saline gargles and fluids as prescribed.
  • Begin prescribed antiviral therapy within 24 to 48 hr of symptom onset.
  • Teach annual influenza vaccination, hand hygiene, cough etiquette, increased fluids/rest if symptoms occur, and avoiding close contact or crowds during outbreaks.

Pneumonia: Assess, Culture, Oxygenate, Treat

Lower airway

Assessment and diagnostics

  • Pneumonia fills alveoli with inflammatory fluid or exudate and can be triggered by infection or aspiration.
  • Community-acquired pneumonia is common and can follow influenza; health care-associated pneumonia has higher mortality and can be more antibiotic-resistant.
  • Expected findings include fever, chills, flushed face, diaphoresis, dyspnea, tachypnea, pleuritic chest pain, sputum, crackles, wheezes, cough, dull percussion, and low oxygen saturation.
  • Older adults can have vague findings; confusion from hypoxia can be the most common manifestation.
  • Obtain sputum culture before antibiotics when possible, and monitor CBC, ABGs, blood culture, electrolytes, chest x-ray, and pulse oximetry.

Nursing care and complications

  • Position in high-Fowler's unless contraindicated, encourage coughing or suctioning, administer oxygen, breathing treatments, and medications, and monitor skin under oxygen devices.
  • Encourage incentive spirometry, rest periods, nutrition, and 2 to 3 L/day of fluids if not contraindicated to thin secretions and support healing.
  • Give antibiotics after cultures are obtained; monitor stool frequency and kidney function, especially in older adults receiving penicillins or cephalosporins.
  • Monitor bronchodilator and steroid effects, including albuterol tremor/tachycardia, ipratropium dry mouth or palpitations, theophylline toxicity, hyperglycemia, fluid retention, and poor wound healing.
  • Watch for atelectasis, bacteremia/sepsis, and ARDS when hypoxemia persists despite oxygen therapy.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Rhinitis

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse in a clinic is discussing health promotion and disease management with a client who has rhinitis.

Risk factors and findings

  • Risk factors include recent viral, bacterial, or influenza exposure; lack of current immunizations; allergens or environmental contaminants; tobacco smoke; substance use; foreign body; and immobility.
  • Expected findings include rhinorrhea, nasal congestion, purulent nasal drainage, sneezing, pruritus of the nose/throat/ears, watery eyes, sore dry throat, inflamed nasal mucosa, and low-grade fever.

Teaching and medications

  • Teach rest, increased fluids if not contraindicated, humidification or steamy air, tissue disposal, and cough etiquette.
  • Medication examples include brompheniramine/pseudoephedrine, cromolyn sodium, phenylephrine, and antipyretics as appropriate.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 21

Asthma

Asthma is intermittent, reversible airway obstruction driven by inflammation, hyperresponsiveness, bronchoconstriction, mucosal edema, and mucus production. The nurse's job is to read airflow decline early and match rescue versus prevention therapy.

TL;DR - Rescue Fast, Prevent Daily, Escalate Early

Asthma worsens when wheezing, retractions, tachycardia, low SaO2, or rising PaCO2 appear. Use short-acting beta2 agonists for rapid relief, anti-inflammatory therapy for prevention, peak flow zones for self-management, and prepare for intubation if respiratory failure or status asthmaticus develops.

Trigger Control

  • Promote smoking cessation, infection prevention, influenza/pneumonia vaccines, and protective masks or ventilation in dusty or particle-heavy environments.
  • Teach avoidance of cold air shifts, humidity changes, pollutants, strong odors, seasonal/perennial allergens, stress, aspirin/NSAIDs, beta-blockers, cholinergics, detergents, cleaners, sinusitis/postnasal drip, and viral infections when relevant.
  • Exercise is part of therapy, but some clients need premedication before activity.

Red Flags

  • Early ABGs can show low PaO2 with low PaCO2 from hyperventilation; later high PaCO2 signals fatigue and worsening ventilation.
  • Status asthmaticus is life-threatening and can be unresponsive to usual treatment.
  • For severe obstruction, prepare for emergency intubation and give prescribed IV fluids, oxygen, bronchodilators, epinephrine, and systemic steroids.

Asthma Care Map

Assess Severity and Identify Triggers

Airflow obstruction

Classification and risk

  • Asthma is classified by symptom frequency: mild intermittent, mild persistent, moderate persistent, or severe persistent.
  • Risk factors include family history, smoking, secondhand smoke, environmental allergies, chemical irritants or dust, GERD, and age-related loss of pulmonary reserve.
  • Older adults have decreased pulmonary reserve, more infection susceptibility, and reduced beta-adrenergic receptor sensitivity, which can blunt response to beta agonists.

Expected and declining findings

  • Expected asthma manifestations include dyspnea, chest tightness, anxiety, coughing, wheezing, mucus production, accessory muscle use, prolonged exhalation, low SaO2, and increased chest diameter.
  • Ask about onset, duration, triggers, medication changes, relief medications, other medications, and self-care used during current and prior exacerbations.
  • Wheezing, sternal retractions, tachycardia, worsening oxygenation, and increased work of breathing show declining status.

Diagnostics and Acute Nursing Care

Priority response

Tests

  • ABGs can show hypoxemia, hypocarbia early in an attack, and hypercarbia later in an attack.
  • Sputum culture can identify infection when bacteria are present.
  • PFTs are the most accurate test for diagnosing asthma and severity; FEV1 can drop below expected values, and improvement after bronchodilator therapy supports asthma diagnosis.
  • Chest x-ray can show structural changes over time.

Acute care

  • Position in high-Fowler's to maximize ventilation.
  • Administer prescribed oxygen and medications, initiate/maintain IV access, and monitor heart rhythm, respiratory effort, symmetry, SaO2, and lung sounds.
  • Stay calm and reassuring; anxiety can worsen air hunger.
  • Provide rest periods and safe walking paths for older adults with dyspnea.
  • Encourage prompt treatment of infections and appropriate immunizations.

Medication Logic: Rescue Versus Prevention

Pharmacology

Bronchodilators

  • Short-acting beta2 agonists, such as albuterol, provide rapid relief for acute manifestations and can prevent exercise-induced asthma.
  • Anticholinergics, such as ipratropium, support bronchodilation and decrease secretions; monitor dry mouth and heart rate.
  • Theophylline has a narrow therapeutic range and is used only when other treatments are ineffective; monitor levels and toxicity signs such as tachycardia, nausea, and diarrhea.
  • Long-acting beta2 agonists, such as salmeterol, are for prevention, not rescue at attack onset.

Anti-inflammatory agents

  • Corticosteroids, leukotriene antagonists, mast cell stabilizers, and monoclonal antibodies decrease airway inflammation and are used for prophylaxis.
  • Monitor for decreased immune function, poor wound healing, hyperglycemia, fluid retention, weight gain, mouth/throat lesions, and anaphylaxis with omalizumab.
  • Teach prednisone with food, hydration, black/tarry stool reporting, hand and mouth care, infection avoidance, and no abrupt discontinuation.
  • If separate inhaled bronchodilator and anti-inflammatory agents are both prescribed at the same time, give the bronchodilator first to improve absorption of the anti-inflammatory medication.

Complications: Respiratory Failure and Status Asthmaticus

Escalation

Respiratory failure

  • Persistent hypoxemia from asthma can progress to respiratory failure.
  • Monitor oxygenation and acid-base balance closely.
  • Prepare for intubation and mechanical ventilation when ventilation or oxygenation cannot be maintained.

Status asthmaticus

  • Status asthmaticus is a life-threatening airway obstruction episode that can be unresponsive to common treatment.
  • Findings include extreme wheezing, labored breathing, accessory muscle use, distended neck veins, and risk for cardiac or respiratory arrest.
  • Prepare for emergency intubation and administer prescribed IV fluids, oxygen, bronchodilators, epinephrine, and systemic steroids.

Active Learning Scenario

Medication - Prednisone for Asthma

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is caring for a client who has asthma and a prescription for prednisone.

Nursing interventions

  • Monitor for decreased immune function and delayed wound healing.
  • Monitor blood glucose for hyperglycemia.
  • Observe for fluid retention and weight gain.
  • Inspect the mouth and throat for aphthous lesions.

Client teaching

  • Take prednisone with food to reduce GI distress.
  • Report black, tarry stools.
  • Maintain hydration, hand hygiene, and mouth care.
  • Do not stop glucocorticoids suddenly without provider guidance.

1

  • A. SaO2 95%.
  • B. Wheezing.
  • C. Retraction of sternal muscles.
  • D. Pink mucous membranes.
  • E. Tachycardia.
B, C, E Wheezing, sternal retractions, and tachycardia indicate airway narrowing, increased work of breathing, and decreased oxygenation.
A, D SaO2 95% and pink mucous membranes are expected findings, not signs of decline.
2

  • A. Antibiotic.
  • B. Beta-blocker.
  • C. Antiviral.
  • D. Beta2 agonist.
D A beta2 agonist rapidly dilates bronchioles to relieve wheezing and open the airway.
A, B, C Antibiotics treat bacterial infection, antivirals treat viral infection, and beta-blockers can worsen bronchospasm risk.

  • D. "I will monitor for weight loss while taking this medication."
C Prednisone should be taken with food to reduce GI distress.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 22

Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease

COPD is irreversible airflow limitation from emphysema, chronic bronchitis, or both. Care centers on controlled oxygen, secretion clearance, nutrition, breathing techniques, medication safety, infection prevention, and support for long-term lifestyle change.

TL;DR - Clear Secretions, Controlled Oxygen, Conserve Energy

COPD clients need high-Fowler's positioning, effective coughing or suctioning, deep breathing, incentive spirometry, prescribed oxygen usually targeting 88% to 92%, high-calorie nutrition, fluids to thin secretions, pursed-lip breathing, and infection prevention.

Recognize COPD

  • Emphysema destroys alveoli, reduces gas-exchange surface area, traps air, and causes CO2 retention and respiratory acidosis.
  • Chronic bronchitis is chronic bronchial and bronchiolar inflammation from long-term irritant exposure.
  • Smoking is the primary risk factor; also assess age, AAT deficiency, and environmental pollution exposure.

Prevent Deterioration

  • Administer oxygen as prescribed and monitor closely; clients with chronic CO2 retention can require lower-flow oxygen.
  • Teach pursed-lip breathing: breathe in through the nose and exhale slowly through pursed lips without puffing the cheeks.
  • Report infection concerns early and maintain influenza and pneumonia immunizations.

COPD Care Map

Pathophysiology, Risk, and Findings

Irreversible obstruction

Disease pattern

  • COPD includes emphysema and chronic bronchitis; most clients with emphysema also have chronic bronchitis.
  • Emphysema causes loss of elasticity, hyperinflation, alveolar destruction, decreased gas-exchange surface area, CO2 retention, and respiratory acidosis.
  • Chronic bronchitis is inflammation of bronchi and bronchioles from chronic irritant exposure.
  • COPD usually affects middle-age to older adults.

Assessment clues

  • Findings include chronic dyspnea, exertional dyspnea, productive morning cough, hypoxemia, crackles, wheezes, rapid shallow respirations, accessory muscle use, irregular breathing, and decreased oxygen saturation.
  • Emphysema can show barrel chest, hyperresonance from trapped air, thin extremities, and enlarged neck muscles.
  • Late or advanced findings include clubbing, pallor/cyanosis, and dependent edema from right-sided heart failure.
  • During acute exacerbations, respiratory rate can reach 40 to 50/min.

Diagnostics and Core Nursing Care

Controlled support

Diagnostics

  • Increased hematocrit can reflect chronic low oxygenation.
  • Sputum cultures and WBC counts help diagnose acute respiratory infection.
  • ABGs can show hypoxemia and hypercarbia.
  • PFTs classify COPD severity with FEV/FVC comparisons; as COPD advances, the ratio decreases.
  • Chest x-ray can show hyperinflation and flattened diaphragm in late emphysema, and AAT levels assess for deficiency.

Nursing care

  • Position in high-Fowler's, encourage effective coughing or suction, deep breathing, incentive spirometry, and medication/breathing treatments.
  • Administer oxygen as prescribed; in many COPD cases, maintain oxygen saturation between 88% and 92%.
  • Monitor skin under oxygen devices.
  • Promote nutrition because work of breathing raises caloric demand; encourage soft, high-calorie foods, rest, and hydration to thin secretions if not contraindicated.
  • Use exercise conditioning with self-paced walking, stopping for dyspnea and resuming after recovery.

Breathing Techniques and Medication Safety

Self-management

Breathing and secretion tools

  • Diaphragmatic breathing: lie supine with knees bent, place a hand over the abdomen for resistance, and verify the hand rises and falls with breathing.
  • Pursed-lip breathing: form lips as if whistling, inhale through the nose, exhale slowly through pursed lips, and do not puff the cheeks.
  • Positive expiratory pressure devices help loosen secretions by vibration during exhalation.
  • Incentive spirometry promotes lung expansion: seal lips around the mouthpiece, inhale deeply, and hold for 3 to 5 seconds.

Medication map

  • Albuterol gives rapid bronchodilation; monitor tremors and tachycardia.
  • Ipratropium decreases pulmonary secretions and helps prevent bronchospasm; monitor dry mouth, heart rate, palpitations, headache, and blurred vision.
  • Theophylline has a narrow therapeutic range; monitor levels and toxicity signs such as tachycardia, nausea, and diarrhea.
  • Corticosteroids and other anti-inflammatory agents decrease airway inflammation; monitor immune function, wound healing, hyperglycemia, hypokalemia, fluid retention, weight gain, mouth lesions, and black/tarry stools.
  • Mucolytics help thin secretions and include nebulized and oral options.

Complications and Discharge Teaching

Long-term care

Complications

  • Respiratory infection can occur from increased mucus and poor oxygenation; monitor oxygenation, WBC, CRP, SaO2, temperature, and infection symptoms.
  • Cor pulmonale occurs when air trapping, airway collapse, and stiff alveoli increase pulmonary pressure and right-sided workload.
  • Manifestations include low oxygenation, cyanosis, enlarged tender liver, distended neck veins, and dependent edema.
  • Monitor respiratory status, GI disturbances, heart rate/rhythm, and fluid balance; administer oxygen and prescribed medications.

Client teaching

  • Eat high-calorie foods, rest as needed, practice hand hygiene, take inhaled/oral medications as prescribed, and stop smoking if needed.
  • Obtain influenza and pneumonia immunizations.
  • Use oxygen as prescribed and keep others from smoking near oxygen because of flammability.
  • Report unusual findings or infection concerns early.
  • Portable oxygen and home services can support independence rather than confine the client to the house.

Active Learning Scenario

Medication - Ipratropium for COPD

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is reviewing discharge instructions for a client who has a new prescription for ipratropium.

Nursing interventions

  • Observe for dry mouth while taking ipratropium.
  • Monitor heart rate because palpitations can indicate toxicity.
  • Assess for headache or blurred vision and reinforce reporting these findings.

Client teaching

  • Suck on hard candies to moisten dry mouth.
  • Increase fluid intake if not contraindicated.
  • Use the medication as prescribed to prevent bronchospasm and support bronchodilation.

  • D. "I can have mouth sores while taking this medication."

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 23

Tuberculosis

Tuberculosis is an airborne infection caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis. It can remain latent for years, become active when host defenses weaken, and requires strict transmission control plus long-course combination therapy.

TL;DR - Airborne Isolation, Multi-Drug Therapy, Full Course

Think TB when a client has prolonged cough, weight loss, fatigue, fever, night sweats, hemoptysis, or risk exposure. Use airborne precautions in care settings, collect early-morning sputum specimens, teach medication adherence for months, and monitor for hepatotoxicity, neurotoxicity, vision changes, and resistant disease.

Transmission Control

  • TB spreads through airborne particles and needs a negative-airflow room in the hospital.
  • Wear an N95 or powered air-purifying respirator when caring for a hospitalized client with TB.
  • If transport is required, place a surgical mask on the client and use the shortest, least busy route.

Therapy Logic

  • Treatment typically starts with multiple antitubercular medications to reduce resistance.
  • Sputum cultures every 2 to 4 weeks help show treatment response.
  • Clients are no longer considered infectious after three consecutive negative sputum cultures.

Tuberculosis Care Map

Transmission, Latency, and Screening

Airborne infection

Pathophysiology

  • After inhalation, the body can wall off the TB organism in the lung, producing a tubercle visible on imaging.
  • Only some exposed clients develop active disease; latent infection can remain dormant for years.
  • TB primarily affects the lungs but can spread through the blood to other organs.
  • Transmission risk decreases after 2 to 3 weeks of effective antitubercular therapy.

Screening priorities

  • Screen close contacts, family members, high-risk communities, clients born outside the United States, migrant workers, and clients living in high-risk areas.
  • Consider TB with persistent cough, chest pain, weakness, weight loss, anorexia, hemoptysis, dyspnea, fever, night sweats, or chills.
  • Completion of treatment is a public-health priority because incomplete therapy promotes resistant TB.

Assessment and Diagnostics

Detect active versus latent

Risk and findings

  • Risk increases with close contact with untreated TB, homelessness or low socioeconomic status, crowded/poorly ventilated housing, advanced age, recent endemic travel, immigration from higher-incidence areas, substance use, immunocompromise, and high-risk health care activities.
  • Expected findings include cough lasting longer than 3 weeks, purulent or blood-streaked sputum, fatigue, lethargy, weight loss, anorexia, afternoon low-grade fever, and night sweats.
  • Older adults can show atypical findings such as altered mentation, behavior change, fever, anorexia, and weight loss.

Tests

  • Nucleic acid amplification testing detects TB organisms in respiratory secretions and can screen for rifampin resistance quickly.
  • QuantiFERON-TB Gold supports diagnosis of latent or active infection and is useful when Mantoux results might be unreliable.
  • Acid-fast bacilli smear suggests active infection, but culture confirms Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
  • Collect three early-morning sputum specimens using PPE and negative-airflow precautions.
  • Mantoux testing is read in 48 to 72 hr; induration thresholds vary by immune status, and a positive result does not by itself confirm active disease.

Nursing Care and Precautions

Protect others

Care setting actions

  • Administer heated, humidified oxygen as prescribed.
  • Implement airborne precautions and place the hospitalized client in a negative-airflow room.
  • Use an N95 or powered air-purifying respirator; add barrier protection if hands or clothing can be contaminated.
  • Teach coughing and sputum expectoration into tissues that the client disposes into bags or no-touch receptacles.

Nutrition and support

  • Administer prescribed medications exactly as ordered.
  • Promote adequate fluids and calories.
  • Encourage protein, iron, vitamin C, and B-vitamin intake.
  • Arrange social services or community clinic follow-up if medication access or monitoring is at risk.

Antitubercular Medication Safety

Resistance prevention

First-line combination

  • A common initial regimen combines isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol.
  • Teach that medications are not interchangeable and the full prescribed course must be completed.
  • Isoniazid is taken on an empty stomach; monitor liver function and signs of hepatotoxicity or peripheral neurotoxicity. Pyridoxine can be prescribed to reduce neurotoxicity risk.
  • Rifampin can turn urine and other secretions orange and can reduce oral contraceptive effectiveness.

High-risk adverse effects

  • Pyrazinamide can cause hepatotoxicity and joint problems; monitor liver enzymes and teach fluids if not contraindicated.
  • Ethambutol requires baseline and ongoing visual acuity and color discrimination checks; report vision changes immediately.
  • Streptomycin is reserved for multidrug-resistant TB because of toxicity risk; monitor hearing and renal status.
  • Avoid alcohol with hepatotoxic TB medications.

Home Teaching and Complications

Follow-through

Home care

  • TB is often managed at home; family members have usually already been exposed, but they still need testing.
  • Continue therapy for the full prescribed duration, which can extend longer for multidrug-resistant disease.
  • Use hand hygiene, cover coughs and sneezes, dispose contaminated tissues in plastic bags, and wear a mask in public while active TB is infectious.
  • Continue follow-up and sputum monitoring until three consecutive negative cultures confirm noninfectious status.

Miliary TB

  • Miliary TB occurs when the organism enters the bloodstream and spreads to multiple organs.
  • Possible complications include life-threatening neurologic findings such as headache, neck stiffness, and drowsiness.
  • Pericarditis can cause dyspnea, neck vein distention, pleuritic pain, and hypotension from impaired cardiac filling.
  • Treatment follows the same core approach as pulmonary TB.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Tuberculosis

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is caring for a client who has tuberculosis and is completing a system disorder template.

Pathophysiology

  • TB is an airborne infection caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
  • The organism can be contained in lung tissue, lie dormant, later become active, and spread outside the lungs through the blood.

Nursing care and complication

  • Administer humidified oxygen, prescribed medications, and nutrition support.
  • Prevent spread with airborne precautions, negative-airflow placement, N95 or powered respirator use, and masked transport if needed.
  • Teach safe tissue disposal and cough hygiene.
  • Monitor for miliary TB, including neurologic findings or pericarditis manifestations.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 24

Pulmonary Embolism

Pulmonary embolism is a medical emergency caused by a solid, gas, or liquid blockage in the pulmonary vasculature. Rapid oxygenation, clot control, diagnostic confirmation, and bleeding surveillance drive safe care.

TL;DR - Oxygen First, Control Clots, Watch Bleeding

PE can present with sudden chest pressure, dyspnea, air hunger, tachycardia, tachypnea, hypotension, low oxygen saturation, pleural friction rub, petechiae, or anxiety. Prioritize oxygen and high-Fowler's positioning, maintain IV access, prepare anticoagulant or thrombolytic therapy, and monitor closely for hemorrhage.

Risk Control

  • Prevent DVT with mobility, leg exercises, compression stockings, hydration, and avoidance of prolonged sitting.
  • Risk increases with immobility, obesity, tobacco use, estrogen therapy, pregnancy, orthopedic surgery, central lines, heart failure, atrial fibrillation, long-bone fracture, cancer, trauma, septicemia, and advanced age.
  • Older adults can decompensate quickly because pulmonary reserve is lower.

Priority Response

  • Use ABC priority: oxygenation comes before diagnostics or anticoagulants when acute dyspnea is present.
  • MDCTA is the criterion standard when available; V/Q scan can be used when contrast is contraindicated.
  • Anticoagulants prevent clot extension; thrombolytics dissolve clot but carry major bleeding risk.

Pulmonary Embolism Care Map

Risk and Presentation

Medical emergency

What blocks flow

  • Most pulmonary emboli originate from venous thromboembolism.
  • Other emboli can involve fat, air, infection-related thrombus, amniotic fluid, tumor, bone marrow, or foreign material.
  • A large embolus impairs pulmonary blood flow and worsens tissue hypoxia.

Expected findings

  • Findings include anxiety, impending doom, sudden chest pressure, inspiratory pain, chest wall tenderness, dyspnea, air hunger, cough, and hemoptysis.
  • Physical findings can include pleurisy, pleural friction rub, tachycardia, hypotension, tachypnea, crackles, S3/S4, diaphoresis, low-grade fever, low SaO2, cyanosis, petechiae over the chest or axillae, distended neck veins, and syncope.

Diagnostics and Priority Nursing Care

Oxygenation first

Tests

  • Early ABGs can show low PaCO2 from hyperventilation and respiratory alkalosis; worsening hypoxemia can progress to respiratory and metabolic acidosis.
  • D-dimer rises when clot formation and fibrin breakdown are present.
  • MDCTA is the criterion standard for PE detection when available.
  • V/Q scan can detect PE and is useful when contrast allergy limits other imaging.
  • Pulmonary angiography can detect PE when MDCTA is unavailable, but it is invasive and higher risk than V/Q scanning.
  • Chest x-ray can support the picture by showing findings such as diaphragm elevation or pleural effusion.

Immediate care

  • Administer oxygen and position in high-Fowler's to maximize ventilation.
  • Initiate and maintain IV access and administer prescribed medications.
  • Assess respiratory status at least every 30 min, including lung sounds, respiratory rate/rhythm/ease, skin color, capillary refill, and tracheal position.
  • Assess cardiac status, compare arm blood pressures, palpate pulse quality, monitor for dysrhythmias, inspect for petechiae, and check for neck vein distention.
  • Provide emotional support and monitor level of consciousness and mental status.

Medication and Procedure Safety

Clot control

Medications

  • Heparin, enoxaparin, and warfarin help prevent the existing clot from enlarging and reduce new clot formation.
  • Monitor PT/INR for warfarin, aPTT for heparin, CBC, and signs of thrombocytopenia, anemia, or hemorrhage.
  • Direct factor Xa inhibitors such as rivaroxaban, apixaban, and fondaparinux require bleeding surveillance.
  • Dabigatran is a direct thrombin inhibitor; assess for bleeding and blood loss.
  • Thrombolytics such as alteplase, reteplase, and tenecteplase dissolve clots but are contraindicated with high bleeding-risk conditions such as active bleeding, recent surgery or trauma, uncontrolled hypertension, pregnancy, history of stroke, or peptic ulcer disease.

Procedures

  • Consult cardiology, pulmonary, respiratory, and radiology services as indicated for PE management, oxygen therapy, ABGs, and diagnostic studies.
  • Embolectomy surgically removes an embolus; prepare with NPO status and informed consent.
  • A vena cava filter can prevent additional emboli from reaching the pulmonary vasculature.
  • After invasive procedures, monitor vital signs, SaO2, incision drainage, and pain.

Prevention, Teaching, and Complications

Bleeding watch

Teaching

  • Encourage smoking cessation, walking, physical activity, compression stockings, avoiding leg crossing, and avoiding prolonged immobility.
  • During travel, stand or walk about 5 min each hour, remain hydrated, wear support stockings, and perform ankle pump exercises.
  • If taking warfarin, keep vitamin K intake consistent, follow PT/INR monitoring instructions, and expect regular blood draws.
  • Reduce bleeding risk with soft toothbrushes, electric razors, gentle nose care, daily skin and mouth checks, and avoidance of aspirin products unless prescribed.

Complications

  • Decreased cardiac output can show as hypotension, tachycardia, cyanosis, neck vein distention, syncope, S3/S4, low urine output, and dysrhythmias.
  • Maintain IV access, monitor urine output, ECG, and pulmonary pressures, and administer prescribed fluids, inotropes, or vasodilators.
  • Hemorrhage risk rises with anticoagulant therapy; assess injection/surgical sites, CBC, PT/aPTT/INR, stool, urine, emesis, abdominal girth, and abdominal or flank pain.
  • Keep the appropriate reversal agent available when ordered or required by facility protocol.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Pulmonary Embolism

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is caring for a client who has a pulmonary embolism and is completing a system disorder template.

Pathophysiology and care

  • A PE occurs when a substance enters venous circulation and blocks pulmonary blood flow; DVT is the most common source.
  • Administer oxygen as prescribed and place the client in high-Fowler's.
  • Maintain IV access, administer prescribed medications, provide emotional support, and monitor mental status.

Medication examples

  • Anticoagulants: enoxaparin, heparin, and warfarin.
  • Thrombolytics: alteplase, reteplase, and tenecteplase.
  • Monitor closely for bleeding with either medication class.

  • D. Elevated platelet count.
A Recent major surgery creates a high hemorrhage risk and is a contraindication to thrombolytic therapy.
B, C, D These are not the thrombolytic contraindications emphasized by the PE source.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

System Disorder, Medication, and Therapeutic Procedure: pulmonary embolism, DVT prevention, MDCTA, V/Q scan, pulmonary angiography, oxygen therapy, heparin, enoxaparin, warfarin, direct Xa inhibitors, dabigatran, thrombolytic therapy, embolectomy, vena cava filter, decreased cardiac output, and hemorrhage. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after source-supported examples are built.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 25

Pneumothorax, Hemothorax, and Flail Chest

Pneumothorax, hemothorax, and flail chest are respiratory emergencies that reduce lung expansion and gas exchange. The nurse watches for distress, supports oxygenation, prepares for decompression or chest tube therapy, and prevents respiratory failure.

TL;DR - Re-Expand the Lung, Relieve Pressure, Watch the Tube

Chest trauma and pleural-space air or blood can rapidly impair ventilation. Prioritize oxygenation, recognize tension signs such as tracheal deviation and worsening distress, prepare for needle decompression or chest tube insertion when indicated, and monitor drainage, pain, oxygenation, and infection signs.

Spot the Emergency

  • Pneumothorax is air in the pleural space causing lung collapse.
  • Hemothorax is blood in the pleural space.
  • Tension pneumothorax traps air under pressure, compresses lung and heart structures, reduces venous return, and can be fatal without rapid treatment.

Do First

  • Administer oxygen and position to maximize ventilation.
  • Prepare emergency decompression for a spontaneous or tension pneumothorax when respiratory function is threatened.
  • After chest tube placement, monitor drainage, system function, dressing, vital signs, SaO2, ABGs, CBC, and chest x-ray results.

Pleural Injury Care Map

Pathophysiology and Assessment

Pleural space emergency

Pleural-space problems

  • Pneumothorax means air or gas in the pleural space collapses lung tissue.
  • Tension pneumothorax occurs when air enters during inspiration and cannot escape on expiration, increasing pressure in the pleural cavity and shifting mediastinal structures.
  • Hemothorax is accumulation of blood in the pleural space.
  • Spontaneous pneumothorax can occur without trauma when a small bleb ruptures.
  • Flail chest occurs when at least two neighboring ribs sustain multiple fractures, creating unstable chest wall movement and impaired expansion.

Assessment cues

  • Risk factors include blunt or penetrating chest trauma, closed or occluded chest tube, older adult pulmonary reserve loss, and COPD.
  • Expected findings include anxiety and pleuritic pain.
  • Respiratory distress can include tachypnea, tachycardia, hypoxia, cyanosis, dyspnea, and accessory muscle use.
  • Pneumothorax can produce reduced or absent breath sounds, asymmetrical movement, hyperresonance, subcutaneous emphysema, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side if tension develops.
  • Hemothorax can produce dull percussion over the affected side.

Diagnostics and Initial Nursing Care

Stabilize breathing

Tests and procedures

  • ABGs can show hypoxemia when PaO2 is less than expected.
  • Chest x-ray confirms pneumothorax or hemothorax.
  • Thoracentesis can help confirm hemothorax; ensure consent, position the client at the bedside, transport specimens, and monitor vital signs, SaO2, and the insertion site.
  • Teach the client to remain still during thoracentesis and avoid moving, coughing, or deep breathing during the procedure.

Priority care

  • Administer oxygen therapy and position in high-Fowler's to maximize ventilation.
  • Auscultate heart and lung sounds and monitor vital signs every 4 hr or more often when unstable.
  • Check ABGs, SaO2, CBC, and chest x-ray results.
  • Provide emotional support to the client and family.
  • Monitor chest tube drainage and administer prescribed medications.

Medication and Chest Tube Safety

Pain and tube management

Medication cautions

  • Benzodiazepines such as lorazepam or midazolam can decrease anxiety, but monitor for hypotension, respiratory distress, amnesia, and paradoxical effects.
  • Opioids such as morphine and fentanyl treat moderate to severe pain but can cause respiratory depression, sedation, euphoria, hypotension, bradypnea, nausea, vomiting, constipation, and fluid retention.
  • Use opioids cautiously for clients who have asthma or emphysema.
  • If respirations are 12/min or less in a client receiving opioids, stop the medication and notify the provider immediately.

Chest tube insertion

  • Chest tubes drain air, fluid, or blood, re-establish negative pressure, support lung expansion, and restore intrapleural pressure.
  • Before insertion, obtain consent, gather supplies, monitor status, and administer pain medication.
  • After insertion, monitor vital signs, SaO2, drainage, tube placement, drainage system function, and dressing.
  • Teach deep breathing, coughing, incentive spirometry, rest periods, hand hygiene, vaccinations, smoking cessation if applicable, and follow-up for fever, cough, breathing difficulty, sharp chest pain, or upper respiratory infection.

Flail Chest

Paradoxical movement

Recognize it

  • Multiple rib fractures from blunt trauma or CPR can create a free-floating chest wall segment.
  • The affected segment moves inward with inspiration and outward with expiration.
  • Expected findings include unequal chest expansion, paradoxical chest movement, tachycardia, hypotension, dyspnea, cyanosis, anxiety, and chest pain.

Support gas exchange

  • Administer humidified oxygen and monitor vital signs and SaO2.
  • Review pulmonary function findings, serial chest x-rays, and ABGs.
  • Assess lung sounds, color, and capillary refill.
  • Promote expansion with positioning and deep breathing.
  • Severe injury can require mechanical ventilation, often using positive pressure to stabilize the flail segment.
  • Administer pain medication, prescribed IV fluids, and suction the trachea or endotracheal tube as needed.

Complications

Perfusion and ventilation

Decreased cardiac output

  • Rising intrathoracic pressure can reduce the amount of blood pumped by the heart.
  • Hypotension can develop as cardiac output falls.
  • Administer prescribed IV fluids or blood products, monitor heart rate and rhythm, and monitor intake/output including chest tube drainage.

Respiratory failure

  • Lung collapse can produce inadequate gas exchange.
  • Prepare for mechanical ventilation if oxygenation or ventilation cannot be maintained.
  • Continue frequent respiratory assessment and escalate new distress quickly.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Hemothorax

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse is teaching a newly licensed nurse about care for a client who has a hemothorax.

Description and nursing care

  • Hemothorax is blood accumulation in the pleural space.
  • Administer oxygen therapy and position in high-Fowler's.
  • Monitor ABGs, SaO2, CBC, chest x-ray findings, heart and lung sounds, vital signs, and chest tube drainage.
  • Document ventilator settings hourly if the client is ventilated.
  • Provide emotional support and encourage prompt care for infection manifestations.

Medication examples

  • Benzodiazepines such as lorazepam or midazolam can reduce anxiety.
  • Opioid agonists such as morphine sulfate or fentanyl can treat moderate to severe pain.
  • Monitor respiratory status closely when sedatives or opioids are used.

Application Exercises

1

A nurse assesses a client after a gunshot wound to the chest. Which findings should the nurse monitor for to detect pneumothorax? Select all that apply.

  • A. Tachypnea.
  • B. Tracheal deviation.
  • C. Bradycardia.
  • D. Decreased accessory muscle use.
  • E. Pleuritic pain.
A, B, E Pneumothorax can cause tachypnea, tracheal deviation as pressure rises, and pleuritic pain.
C, D Tachycardia and increased accessory muscle use are more consistent with respiratory distress from pneumothorax.

  • B. Obtain a large-bore needle for decompression.
  • C. Administer lorazepam.
  • D. Prepare for chest tube insertion.

  • A. "Notify your provider if you experience expected weakness."
  • B. "You should be able to return to work in 1 week."

  • D. "Notify your provider if you develop a productive cough."

  • D. Place the client flat to improve venous return.

Unit 3 · Respiratory Disorders · Chapter 26

Respiratory Failure

Respiratory failure includes acute respiratory failure, acute respiratory distress syndrome, and severe acute respiratory syndrome. Care centers on oxygenation, ventilation, airway protection, medication safety, infection prevention, and early recognition of ventilator complications.

TL;DR - Oxygenate, Ventilate, Prevent Collapse

Respiratory failure is confirmed and trended with ABGs and oxygenation findings. Maintain a patent airway, oxygenate before suctioning, use mechanical ventilation with PEEP or CPAP when needed, monitor for pneumothorax and tube complications, and pair paralysis with sedation and analgesia.

Recognize Failure

  • ARF can be ventilatory failure, oxygenation failure, or both.
  • ARDS involves inflammatory injury to the alveolar-capillary membrane, fluid-filled lung spaces, reduced surfactant, and severe hypoxemia.
  • SARS is a coronavirus-related pulmonary infection spread by respiratory droplets.

Protect the Airway

  • Assess respiratory status at least hourly and more often when unstable.
  • Use PEEP or CPAP to prevent alveolar collapse when prescribed.
  • Monitor for high-pressure alarms, tube displacement, aspiration pneumonia, infection, barotrauma, and decreased cardiac output.

Respiratory Failure Care Map

Types and Risk Factors

ARF, ARDS, SARS

Failure patterns

  • Ventilatory failure can result from lung or chest wall mechanics, respiratory muscle impairment, diaphragm dysfunction, or respiratory control center problems.
  • Oxygenation failure can result from poor pulmonary perfusion, pulmonary edema, pneumonia, or changes in the gas exchange membrane.
  • Combined ventilatory and oxygenation failure produces deeper hypoxemia because gas exchange is poor and carbon dioxide is retained.
  • Older adults can decompensate quickly because pulmonary reserve is lower.

High-risk conditions

  • ARF risks include COPD, pulmonary embolism, pneumothorax, flail chest, ARDS, asthma, pulmonary edema, lung fibrosis, neuromuscular disorders, spinal cord injury, stroke, elevated intracranial pressure, pneumonia, hypoventilation, shock, low hemoglobin, carbon monoxide poisoning, high altitude, and smoke inhalation.
  • ARDS risks include shock, DIC, aspiration, pulmonary emboli, pneumonia, sepsis, near-drowning, trauma, multiple blood transfusions, CNS damage, smoke or toxic gas inhalation, and drug toxicity.
  • SARS risk increases after exposure to an infected person or immunocompromised status.

Assessment and Diagnostics

ABG-driven

Findings

  • Expected findings include shortness of breath, dyspnea, orthopnea, rapid shallow breathing, cyanotic/mottled/dusky skin or mucous membranes, tachycardia, hypotension, retractions, SaO2 less than 90%, adventitious sounds, dysrhythmias, confusion, and lethargy.
  • ARF can cause dyspnea, orthopnea, cyanosis, pallor, hypoxemia, tachycardia, confusion, irritability, agitation, restlessness, and hypercarbia.
  • ARDS can cause dyspnea, noncardiogenic pulmonary edema with crackles, reduced lung compliance, dense bilateral infiltrates, and severe hypoxemia despite 100% oxygen.

Tests

  • ABGs confirm and monitor ARF, ARDS, and SARS.
  • Respiratory failure criteria include PaO2 less than expected with oxygen saturation less than 90% on room air, or PaCO2 greater than expected with acidemia.
  • Chest x-ray can show pulmonary edema, cardiomegaly, diffuse infiltrates, white-out or ground-glass ARDS appearance, or SARS infiltrates.
  • ECG helps rule out cardiac involvement.
  • Hemodynamic monitoring supports fluid management; pulmonary capillary wedge pressure in ARDS is usually low or within expected range.

Airway, Ventilation, and Supportive Care

Prevent hypoxemia

Oxygenation care

  • Maintain a patent airway and monitor respiratory status every hour and more often as needed.
  • Mechanical ventilation with PEEP or CPAP can be required to prevent alveolar collapse during expiration.
  • Oxygenate before suctioning secretions to prevent worsening hypoxemia.
  • Suction as needed and document sputum color, amount, and consistency.
  • Monitor for pneumothorax because high PEEP can collapse the lungs.
  • Obtain ABGs as prescribed and after ventilator setting adjustments.
  • Use continuous ECG monitoring for hypoxemia-related changes, especially during repositioning and suctioning.

Infection, nutrition, communication

  • Prevent infection with hand hygiene, appropriate suctioning technique, oral care every 2 hr and as needed, and protective clothing when indicated.
  • Promote nutrition by assessing bowel sounds, monitoring elimination, daily weights, intake/output, and prescribed enteral or parenteral feedings.
  • Prevent aspiration with enteral feedings by elevating the head of the bed 30 to 45 degrees and confirming NG tube placement before feeding.
  • Provide emotional support and alternative communication tools such as a dry erase board, pen, or paper.

Medication Safety

Sedation and paralysis

Sedation and pain

  • Benzodiazepines such as lorazepam or midazolam reduce anxiety, resistance to ventilation, and oxygen consumption; monitor respirations when not mechanically ventilated, blood pressure, and SaO2.
  • Propofol sedates intubated and ventilated clients; monitor ECG, blood pressure, sedation level, and hypotension, and remember it has no analgesic action.
  • Opioids such as morphine or fentanyl manage pain; monitor respirations, blood pressure, heart rate, SaO2, ABGs, pain response, and have naloxone and resuscitation equipment available for severe respiratory depression when not ventilated.

Inflammation, paralysis, infection

  • Corticosteroids reduce inflammation; taper gradually, give with antiulcer protection as prescribed, monitor weight, blood pressure, glucose, and electrolytes, and teach oral doses with food.
  • Neuromuscular blockers such as vecuronium, atracurium, or rocuronium facilitate ventilation and decrease oxygen consumption but do not sedate or relieve pain.
  • Give pain medication and sedatives with neuromuscular blockers, monitor ECG/blood pressure/muscle strength, keep reversal medications and resuscitation equipment available, and explain that paralysis is medication-induced.
  • Culture sputum before the first antibiotic dose when antibiotics are prescribed for identified organisms; monitor hypersensitivity, IV site, renal function, and coagulopathy.

Ventilation Procedures and Complications

Tube and pressure risks

Intubation and PEEP

  • Before intubation, oxygenate with 100% oxygen, assist ventilation with a manual resuscitation bag and face mask, and keep emergency equipment ready.
  • After intubation, confirm placement with end-tidal carbon dioxide, bilateral lung sounds, symmetrical chest movement, and chest x-ray findings.
  • Secure the endotracheal tube and assess the balloon cuff for leaks periodically.
  • PEEP applies pressure at the end of expiration to keep alveoli expanded, increase oxygenation, and improve lung expansion.
  • Kinetic therapy rotates the client laterally to reduce atelectasis and improve ventilation; increase rotation gradually and stop if distress develops.

Complications

  • Endotracheal tube trauma can injure the trachea or vocal cords; long-term ventilation can require tracheostomy.
  • Check tube position every 1 to 2 hr and after movement, transfer, or turning; assess lung sounds, SaO2, and chest movement.
  • Prevent aspiration pneumonia by checking cuff leaks, suction contents, and NG tube placement.
  • A high-pressure alarm can signal a blocked tube or pneumothorax; suction secretions or insert an oral airway if biting is causing obstruction.
  • PEEP can increase intrathoracic pressure, reduce venous return and cardiac output, cause hypotension, and lower urine output; monitor intake/output, weight, and hydration.
  • Positive-pressure ventilation can cause barotrauma such as pneumothorax or subcutaneous emphysema.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse reviews the plan of care for a client who has ARDS.

Risk factors

  • ARDS can follow aspiration, pulmonary emboli, pneumonia, other pulmonary infections, sepsis, near-drowning, trauma, CNS damage, smoke or toxic gas inhalation, and drug toxicity.
  • Localized lung injury or systemic problems can trigger the inflammatory process.

Oxygenation care and complications

  • Maintain a patent airway, monitor respiratory status, suction as needed, assess lung sounds, document sputum, and oxygenate before suctioning.
  • Use mechanical ventilation and PEEP as prescribed, monitor for pneumothorax, obtain ABGs after ventilator setting changes, and maintain continuous ECG monitoring.
  • Watch for endotracheal tube trauma, aspiration pneumonia, infection, blocked or displaced tube, increased intrathoracic pressure, decreased cardiac output, and barotrauma.

  • A. Obtain a chest x-ray.
  • B. Prepare for chest tube insertion.
  • C. Administer oxygen through a high-flow mask.
  • D. Initiate IV access.

  • C. "This medication decreases inflammation."
  • D. "This medication reduces anxiety."

  • B. Provide supplemental oxygen.

  • E. Maintain ventilatory support when needed.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 27

Cardiovascular Diagnostic and Therapeutic Procedures

Cardiovascular procedures evaluate myocardial injury, perfusion, fluid status, cardiac workload, valve function, and vessel patency. Nursing priorities are preparation, consent, monitoring, contrast and bleeding precautions, line integrity, and rapid recognition of complications.

TL;DR - Prep the Test, Level the Line, Watch the Site

Know what each cardiovascular test answers: enzymes show injury timing, lipid profile shows risk, echocardiography shows structure and ejection fraction, stress testing shows workload tolerance, hemodynamic monitoring shows volume/perfusion, angiography shows vessel blockage, and vascular access supports therapy. After invasive procedures, watch pulses, bleeding, hematoma, renal function, dysrhythmias, and infection.

Before Procedures

  • Verify consent when required and explain expected sensations or restrictions.
  • Check fasting instructions, contrast allergy, renal function, and metformin guidance when angiography is planned.
  • For hemodynamic monitoring, purge air, keep the system sterile, level at the phlebostatic axis, and zero to atmospheric pressure.

After Procedures

  • Confirm line placement with chest x-ray when indicated.
  • Compare trends with physical assessment instead of treating one number in isolation.
  • Report bleeding, pulse/color/temperature changes, chest pain, dyspnea, waveform changes, infection, or resistance during flushing.

Procedure Care Map

Labs, Echocardiography, and Stress Testing

Diagnostic basics

Cardiac labs and echo

  • Cardiac enzymes rise when myocardial ischemia injures heart muscle; troponins remain elevated longer than myoglobin and CK-MB.
  • Lipid profile supports early heart-disease risk detection; fasting is recommended before lipid sampling.
  • Transthoracic echocardiography is noninvasive and helps diagnose valve disorders, cardiomyopathy, heart failure, angina or MI effects, heart structure/motion, and ejection fraction.
  • Transesophageal echocardiography gives clearer images through the esophagus; verify consent, NPO status, IV access, sedation monitoring, gag reflex return, and head-of-bed elevation afterward.

Stress testing

  • Exercise stress testing evaluates how the heart responds to workload while the client walks on a treadmill.
  • Medication stress testing can be used if fatigue or disability prevents treadmill completion.
  • Before testing, obtain consent, recommend comfortable shoes and clothing, follow fasting instructions, avoid tobacco/alcohol/caffeine, and encourage rest the night before.
  • During testing, use 12-lead ECG monitoring, watch for dysrhythmias, and tell the client to report chest pain, shortness of breath, or dizziness.
  • After testing, continue ECG and blood pressure monitoring until stable.

Hemodynamic Monitoring

Pressure trends

What it measures

  • Hemodynamic monitoring uses indwelling catheters to evaluate blood volume, perfusion, fluid status, and cardiac pumping.
  • Parameters can include CVP, PAP, PAWP, cardiac output, intra-arterial blood pressure, and mixed venous oxygen saturation.
  • Arterial lines give continuous blood pressure data and allow arterial blood sampling, but are not used for IV fluids.
  • PA catheters can measure right atrial pressure, pulmonary artery pressures, PA wedge pressure, cardiac output, and mixed venous oxygenation depending on port/component.

Nursing actions

  • Assemble the pressure system, purge air, maintain sterile connections, position the client, administer prescribed sedation/pain medication, and level at the phlebostatic axis.
  • Zero the system to atmospheric pressure and compare arterial pressure with noninvasive blood pressure.
  • After insertion, confirm placement with chest x-ray, monitor vital signs/rhythm/SaO2, observe waveforms, secure connections, and document catheter placement.
  • Before readings, position supine with head of bed up to 15 to 30 degrees, level and zero the transducer, compare values to physical assessment, and trend values over time.
  • In older adults, reduced intravascular volume can lower hemodynamic values, especially with dehydration.

Angiography and PCI Safety

Contrast and access site

Before and during

  • Coronary angiography evaluates the presence, location, and severity of coronary artery blockage using contrast under fluoroscopy.
  • Before angiography, maintain NPO status, obtain vital signs, assess heart/lung sounds and peripheral pulses, verify consent, explain the procedure, assess iodine/shellfish allergy, and check renal function.
  • Ask the provider about holding metformin before and after contrast exposure.
  • Teach that a mild sedative and local anesthetic can be used, warmth or flushing can occur when dye is injected, pressure is held afterward, and the affected extremity may need to remain straight.
  • During the procedure, administer prescribed sedation/analgesia, monitor vital signs/rhythm/chest pain, and be ready for dysrhythmia or resuscitation needs.

After and complications

  • After angiography, monitor vital signs and affected extremity for bleeding, hematoma, thrombosis, pedal pulse, color, and temperature at prescribed intervals.
  • Maintain bed rest in supine position with the extremity straight for the prescribed time, monitor for dysrhythmias, hydrate for contrast excretion, and assist with sheath removal as directed.
  • Watch for cardiac tamponade, hematoma, allergic reaction, external bleeding, embolism, restenosis, retroperitoneal bleeding, and acute kidney injury.
  • Teach reportable findings: bleeding, chest pain, shortness of breath, color/temperature changes, flank pain, and recurrent cardiac manifestations.
  • If a stent is placed, emphasize prescribed antiplatelet therapy and follow-up laboratory monitoring.

Central Vascular Access

Line integrity

Device selection

  • Central IV catheters support therapies that require rapid hemodilution, long-term access, blood, chemotherapy, antibiotics, or parenteral nutrition.
  • Nontunneled CVCs are short-term lines placed in large central veins with tips in the distal SVC.
  • Tunneled catheters support long-term access and create a tissue barrier between skin exit and vein entry.
  • PICCs are inserted through basilic or cephalic veins and can remain for extended therapy when the tip is positioned in the lower SVC.
  • Implanted ports are surgically placed reservoirs used for long-term access and require trained access with a noncoring needle.

Care and complications

  • Before central access insertion, verify consent, cleanse with chlorhexidine, ensure sterile equipment, and restrict room entry as needed.
  • After PICC insertion, confirm placement by x-ray, assess site/dressing, scrub access ports with alcohol for 15 seconds and allow drying, use transparent dressing, protect the site from water, and avoid BP/venipuncture in the PICC arm.
  • Use a 10 mL syringe for PICC flushing and never force fluid against resistance.
  • Monitor for phlebitis, occlusion, mechanical complications, port dislodgement, catheter tip movement, and infection.
  • Report swelling, unrestricted port movement, inability to access the port, gurgling/swooshing sounds, or pain in the neck/ear on the affected side.

Active Learning Scenario

Diagnostic Procedure - Cardiac Exercise Stress Test

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse reviews the plan of care for a client scheduled for a cardiac exercise stress test.

Description and indications

  • The client exercises the cardiac muscle by walking on a treadmill so the team can evaluate cardiac workload response.
  • Indications include angina, heart failure, myocardial infarction evaluation, and dysrhythmia evaluation.

Key interventions

  • Obtain informed consent and explain treadmill expectations, comfortable clothing, and athletic shoes.
  • Explain pharmacologic stress testing if the client cannot complete treadmill exercise.
  • Teach fasting and avoidance of tobacco, alcohol, and caffeine per policy, and encourage rest the night before.
  • Monitor heart rate and rhythm with a 12-lead ECG and instruct the client to report chest pain, shortness of breath, or dizziness.

A client has a PICC in the right arm. Which interventions should the nurse include? Select all that apply.
