  • B. Apply gentle force if resistance is met.
  • C. Cleanse ports with alcohol for 15 seconds before use.
  • D. Maintain a transparent dressing over the insertion site.
  • E. Flush with 10 mL heparin before and after medication administration.
A, C, D Use a 10 mL syringe, scrub ports with alcohol and allow drying, and maintain transparent dressing for site visualization and infection prevention.
B, E Do not force against resistance; flush with 0.9% sodium chloride around medications, with heparin used when not actively in use per line type/facility protocol.

Related ATI Templates

Suggested template type

Diagnostic Procedure, Therapeutic Procedure, Nursing Skill, and Basic Concept: cardiac enzymes, lipid profile, transthoracic echocardiography, transesophageal echocardiography, stress testing, hemodynamic monitoring, arterial line care, PA catheter care, angiography, PCI, cardiac tamponade, contrast reaction, retroperitoneal bleeding, central venous catheter, PICC, implanted port, phlebitis, and occlusion. Worked AMS Templates will be linked after source-supported examples are built.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 28

Electrocardiography and Dysrhythmia Monitoring

Electrocardiography records cardiac electrical activity through skin electrodes and helps detect dysrhythmias, ischemia, injury, infarction, chamber enlargement, electrolyte effects, and medication effects. Dysrhythmia care depends on rhythm, symptoms, perfusion, and whether a pulse is present.

TL;DR - Read the Rhythm, Treat Perfusion, Clear Before Shock

ECG monitoring does not replace client reporting of chest pain or dyspnea. Prep clean skin and correct lead placement, analyze rate/rhythm/P waves/PR/QRS/ST/T/QT, treat serious dysrhythmias rapidly, synchronize cardioversion when a pulse is present and indicated, and defibrillate ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia.

Monitoring Logic

  • Continuous monitoring requires proximity to the monitor; telemetry allows ambulation while monitored.
  • Clients must still report shortness of breath, chest pain, or worsening acute coronary symptoms.
  • Older adults can show dysrhythmia findings mainly with increased activity.

Shock Safety

  • Cardioversion is synchronized to the QRS complex.
  • Defibrillation is unsynchronized and used for ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia.
  • Everyone must stand clear of the client, bed, and connected equipment before shock delivery.

ECG and Dysrhythmia Care Map

Electrocardiography and Monitoring

Electrical activity

Purpose

  • ECG records electrical activity over time using leads connected to skin electrodes on the chest and limbs.
  • Monitoring can be standard 12-lead ECG, ambulatory Holter, continuous cardiac monitoring, or telemetry.
  • Cardiac monitoring helps diagnose dysrhythmias, chamber enlargement, myocardial ischemia/injury/infarction, electrolyte effects, and medication effects.
  • Dysrhythmias are disturbances in beat formation, conduction, or myocardial response.

Preparation and teaching

  • Position supine with chest exposed, wash skin to remove oils, and clip hair if needed rather than shaving.
  • Place limb electrodes on flat surfaces above wrists and ankles and place six electrodes on the chest while avoiding chest hair.
  • During ECG, monitor for chest pain, decreased level of consciousness, shortness of breath, dysrhythmia manifestations, and hypoxia.
  • Teach the client to remain still and breathe normally during a 12-lead ECG.
  • After ECG, remove leads, print the report, notify the provider, apply Holter monitoring if indicated, and continue dysrhythmia monitoring.

Rhythm Analysis and Dysrhythmia Risk

Perfusion risk

Analyze the strip

  • Determine heart rate and whether rhythm is regular or irregular.
  • Analyze P-wave regularity and shape.
  • Measure PR interval for consistency; expected range is 0.12 to 0.20 seconds.
  • Measure QRS duration and consistency of appearance.
  • Inspect ST segment because depression or elevation is unexpected.
  • Assess T wave and measure QT interval.

Risk and significance

  • Dysrhythmias can be classified by site of origin or by rate/rhythm effect such as bradycardia, tachycardia, heart block, premature beat, flutter, fibrillation, or asystole.
  • Electrophysiology studies can identify the area causing the dysrhythmia and can support ablation.
  • Life-threatening effects are usually related to decreased cardiac output and ineffective tissue perfusion.
  • Risk/presentation factors include cardiovascular disease, MI, hypoxia, acid-base imbalance, electrolyte disturbance, kidney/liver/lung disease, pericarditis, drug or alcohol use, hypovolemia, and shock.
  • Rapid recognition and treatment are essential because dysrhythmias can cause sudden death, especially in acute MI.

Medication and Electrical Treatment Map

ACLS-aligned

Common treatment direction

  • Symptomatic bradycardia can require atropine, dopamine or epinephrine infusion if unresponsive, and pacemaker support.
  • Atrial fibrillation, supraventricular tachycardia, or ventricular tachycardia with a pulse can require antidysrhythmic medications and synchronized cardioversion.
  • Ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia requires rapid defibrillation and emergency medications.
  • Management follows ACLS evidence-based protocols and depends on rhythm, pulse, symptoms, and perfusion.

Cardioversion versus defibrillation

  • Cardioversion delivers a synchronized countershock timed to the QRS complex.
  • Cardioversion is used for symptomatic atrial dysrhythmias, supraventricular tachycardia, and ventricular tachycardia with a pulse.
  • Defibrillation delivers an unsynchronized countershock to stop electrical activity and allow a perfusing rhythm to restart.
  • Defibrillation is used for ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia.

Cardioversion and Defibrillation Nursing Care

Shock procedure safety

Before and during

  • For atrial fibrillation of unknown duration, adequate anticoagulation before cardioversion helps prevent thrombus dislodgement.
  • Before elective cardioversion, explain the procedure, obtain consent, administer oxygen, document baseline rhythm, have emergency equipment available, and hold digoxin as prescribed.
  • Administer prescribed sedation, confirm lead placement and energy settings, and monitor in a lead that gives an upright QRS complex.
  • Activate synchronization for cardioversion; failure to synchronize can trigger lethal dysrhythmia.
  • All staff must stand clear of the client, equipment, and bed before shock delivery.

After and complications

  • After cardioversion or defibrillation, monitor vital signs, airway patency, and ECG.
  • Document postprocedure rhythm, number of attempts, energy settings, time, response, condition, consciousness, and skin condition under electrodes.
  • Teach pulse checks and reporting of palpitations or irregularities.
  • Monitor for pulmonary or systemic emboli after cardioversion, including PE, stroke, or MI findings.
  • Monitor for decreased cardiac output and heart failure manifestations such as hypotension, syncope, tachycardia, dyspnea, productive cough, edema, and venous distention.

Active Learning Scenario

Therapeutic Procedure - Electrocardiography

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse educator reviews electrocardiography with a group of nurses.

Description and indications

  • Electrocardiography records the heart's electrical activity over time through skin electrodes on the chest and limbs.
  • Continuous monitoring requires proximity to the monitor, while telemetry allows ambulation.
  • Identifiable rhythms include sinus bradycardia/tachycardia, AV blocks, atrial fibrillation, SVT, ventricular fibrillation, asystole, PVCs, and PACs.

Nursing interventions

  • Preprocedure: position supine, expose chest, clean skin, and apply limb/chest electrodes while avoiding or clipping hair as needed.
  • Intraprocedure: tell the client to remain still and breathe normally; monitor for dysrhythmia manifestations and hypoxia.
  • Postprocedure: remove leads, print the ECG, notify the provider, apply Holter monitoring if needed, and continue monitoring for dysrhythmia and hypoxia.

Application Exercises

1

Which clients are at risk for dysrhythmia development? Select all that apply.

  • A. A client with metabolic alkalosis.
  • B. A client with potassium 4.3 mEq/L.
  • C. A client with SaO2 96%.
  • D. A client with COPD.
  • E. A client after coronary artery stent placement.
A, D, E Acid-base imbalance, lung disease with hypoxia risk, and cardiac disease/procedure history increase dysrhythmia risk.
B, C Potassium 4.3 mEq/L and SaO2 96% are within expected ranges and do not add the risk identified here.
2

A client with atrial fibrillation for 3 years is scheduled for electrical cardioversion and reports no anticoagulant therapy in the past 6 weeks. Which finding should the nurse report?

  • A. Respiratory history.
  • B. Current vital signs.
  • C. Medication history.
  • D. Prescribed morning medications.
C Atrial fibrillation of unknown or prolonged duration requires adequate anticoagulation before cardioversion to reduce clot dislodgement risk.
A, B, D These data can matter, but the missing anticoagulation history is the key cardioversion safety concern.
3

Which items should the nurse document after defibrillation? Select all that apply.

  • A. Follow-up ECG rhythm.
  • B. Energy settings used.
  • C. IV fluid intake.
  • D. Urinary output.
  • E. Skin condition under electrodes.
A, B, E Postprocedure rhythm, energy settings, and skin condition under electrodes are documented after defibrillation/cardioversion.
C, D IV intake and urinary output are not the procedure-specific documentation items emphasized for defibrillation.
4

A telemetry client has heart rate 46/min. Which prescription might be appropriate if symptomatic bradycardia persists?

  • A. Defibrillation.
  • B. Pacemaker insertion.
  • C. Synchronized cardioversion.
  • D. IV lidocaine.
B Symptomatic bradycardia can require pacemaker support.
A, C, D Defibrillation is for ventricular fibrillation or pulseless VT, synchronized cardioversion is for select tachydysrhythmias with a pulse, and lidocaine is not the bradycardia support in this treatment map.
5

During cardioversion, the team leader calls, "Stand clear." What does this mean?

  • A. The cardioverter is charging to the selected setting.
  • B. The team should begin CPR for pulseless electrical activity.
  • C. Team members must not touch the client, bed, or connected equipment.
  • D. A procedure time-out is being performed.
C Standing clear prevents team members from receiving a shock when cardioversion or defibrillation is delivered.
A, B, D Charging, CPR, and time-out language are separate events from the safety command to clear the client and equipment.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 29

Pacemakers and Implantable Cardioverter/Defibrillators

Pacemakers support cardiac rhythm when the heart's natural pacemaker or conduction pathway cannot maintain adequate rate and perfusion. ICDs monitor for life-threatening ventricular rhythms and can deliver therapy automatically, so nursing care focuses on rhythm response, lead stability, bleeding/infection surveillance, and device-safe teaching.

TL;DR - Spike Before Beat, Protect the Lead, Teach the Device

A pacer spike should be followed by the expected atrial or ventricular response. For unstable symptomatic bradycardia, prepare transcutaneous pacing while definitive pacing is arranged. After placement, monitor rhythm against settings, assess pulses and blood pressure, obtain chest imaging for lead and lung complications, restrict shoulder motion as prescribed, and teach daily pulse checks plus magnetic-field and procedure precautions.

Priority checks

  • Pacer spikes should occur before the intended P wave or QRS complex.
  • Compare ECG rhythm and heart rate with prescribed pacemaker settings.
  • Report inadequate perfusion, rhythm mismatch, persistent hiccups, bleeding, hematoma, or infection findings.

Home teaching

  • Carry device identification and tell health care providers, dentists, and airport security personnel about the device.
  • Avoid direct injury to the generator and avoid placing strong magnetic-field items directly over it.
  • Call the provider for dizziness, syncope, fatigue, chest pain, palpitations, dyspnea, weight gain, fever, or hiccups.

Pacemaker and ICD Care Map

Device Types and Modes

Rhythm support

Temporary pacing

  • Transcutaneous pacing uses external pads and can be painful because energy travels through chest tissue; sedation and analgesia are expected for alert clients when possible.
  • Epicardial wires can be placed during open-heart surgery and connected to an external generator if pacing is needed.
  • Transvenous pacing wires are threaded through a central vein into the right atrium, right ventricle, or both.
  • Temporary pacing requires a controlled setting with continuous ECG monitoring.

Permanent pacing and ICDs

  • Permanent pacemakers have an internal generator and are used for chronic or recurrent sinus-node or AV-node dysfunction.
  • Fixed-rate pacing fires at a constant rate regardless of intrinsic electrical activity.
  • Demand pacing senses intrinsic impulses and fires only when the heart's own rate falls below the programmed threshold.
  • An ICD is used for clients at risk for sudden cardiac death or symptomatic ventricular dysrhythmias; it can shock and can also have pacing capability.

Placement and Postprocedure Monitoring

Lead stability

Before insertion

  • Assess understanding of the device and procedure when the situation is not emergent.
  • Verify informed consent when required and available.
  • Prepare skin by cleaning and trimming excess hair; avoid shaving, rubbing, or alcohol on the skin.
  • Teach that temporary wires and an external box must remain dry and that clients must not change pacemaker dials.

After insertion

  • Document insertion time/date, model, settings, rhythm strip, pulse, blood pressure, and client response.
  • Continuously monitor rate and rhythm; compare the ECG with prescribed pacemaker settings and report discrepancies.
  • Obtain chest x-ray to confirm lead placement and evaluate for pneumothorax, hemothorax, or pleural effusion.
  • Provide analgesia, monitor incision bleeding/hematoma/infection, and limit shoulder movement initially as prescribed.
  • Assess for persistent hiccups because diaphragm pacing can signal lead displacement or perforation.

Permanent Device Discharge Teaching

Self-management

Protect the device

  • Carry a device identification card with manufacturer, model, mode, rate parameters, and battery-life information.
  • Wear the sling if prescribed and avoid raising the affected arm above the shoulder for 1 to 2 weeks to prevent lead dislodgement.
  • Avoid contact sports, heavy lifting, and direct blows to the generator site until cleared.
  • Household appliances are generally acceptable, but strong magnetic-field items should not be placed directly over the generator.

Report and plan ahead

  • Check pulse daily at the same time and report a heart rate below the programmed pacemaker rate.
  • Report dizziness, fainting, weakness, chest pain, hiccups, palpitations, difficulty breathing, weight gain, or signs of infection.
  • Tell all providers and dentists about the device because some therapies or tests, including MRI and therapeutic diathermy, can be contraindicated.
  • An ICD shock can feel like a blow to the chest; a person touching the client can feel the impulse, but it is not harmful.

Complications to Catch Early

Unexpected response

Insertion-site and lung complications

  • For infection or hematoma, assess redness, pain, drainage, swelling, CBC, coagulation studies, and prescribed antibiotic needs.
  • For pneumothorax or hemothorax, assess breath sounds, chest movement, oxygen saturation, and chest x-ray results.
  • After transcutaneous pacing, inspect skin under electrodes for burns, especially in older adults, dehydrated clients, or prolonged external pacing.

Pacemaker and ICD problems

  • Improper sensing or pacing can result from settings, lead placement/function, battery status, myocardial damage, or electrolyte imbalance.
  • Pacer spikes on the T wave can trigger life-threatening dysrhythmias.
  • Monitor ECG and blood pressure, keep emergency resuscitation equipment available, and administer antidysrhythmics as prescribed.
  • Inappropriate ICD shocks require device interrogation and possible reprogramming.

Active Learning Scenario

Therapeutic Procedure - Pacemaker Insertion

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A coronary care nurse orients a newly hired nurse to complications related to pacemaker insertion.

Potential complications

  • Infection or hematoma at the insertion site.
  • Pneumothorax or hemothorax after lead placement.
  • Arrhythmias from ventricular irritation or pacemaker malfunction.

Nursing actions

  • Inspect the incision for redness, pain, drainage, swelling, and bleeding; administer antibiotics as prescribed and monitor CBC/coagulation results.
  • Assess breath sounds, chest movement, oxygen saturation, and postprocedure chest x-ray findings.
  • Monitor ECG and blood pressure, administer antidysrhythmics as prescribed, and keep emergency equipment and medications ready.

Application Exercises

1

A client has complete heart block, heart rate 34/min, blood pressure 83/48 mm Hg, lethargy, and difficulty finishing sentences. Which action should the nurse take first?

  • A. Transport the client to the cardiovascular laboratory.
  • B. Prepare the skin for permanent pacemaker insertion.
  • C. Obtain consent for the permanent pacemaker.
  • D. Apply transcutaneous pacemaker pads.
D The immediate threat is inadequate perfusion from severe bradycardia, so external pacing should be started while definitive pacing is arranged.
A, B, C These can be part of permanent pacemaker preparation, but they do not address the unstable rhythm first.
2

A client has a temporary transvenous pacemaker placed through a femoral access site and programmed VVI at 70/min. Which findings should the nurse report? Select all that apply.

  • A. Cool, clammy foot with capillary refill of 5 seconds.
  • B. Pacing spike followed by a QRS complex.
  • C. Persistent hiccups.
  • D. Heart rate 84/min.
  • E. Blood pressure 104/62 mm Hg.
A, C A cool, poorly perfused foot can signal access-site hematoma or vascular compromise, and persistent hiccups can suggest lead perforation with diaphragm stimulation.
B, D, E A spike followed by QRS is expected capture; the listed heart rate and blood pressure are not the concerning findings in this scenario.
3

Which statement by a client with a permanent pacemaker indicates understanding of discharge teaching?

  • A. "I will tell airport screeners that I have a pacemaker."
  • B. "I should expect occasional hiccups after I go home."
  • C. "I will disconnect my garage door opener before using it."
  • D. "I will check my pulse every 2 to 3 days."
A The client should notify airport security personnel and carry device identification.
B, C, D Hiccups should be reported, household items such as garage door openers are generally acceptable, and pulse should be checked daily at the same time.
4

Which statement describes fixed-rate pacemaker mode?

  • A. The pacemaker fires asynchronously.
  • B. The pacemaker fires only when heart rate falls below a set rate.
  • C. The pacemaker automatically adjusts to activity level.
  • D. The pacemaker fires when it senses intrinsic activity.
A Fixed-rate pacing is asynchronous and fires without regard to intrinsic electrical activity.

  • A. Avoid large magnetic fields near the device.
  • B. Warn family that an ICD shock is harmful if they touch the client.
  • C. Check body temperature at the same time each day.
  • D. Wear tight clothing over the device to keep it in place.
  • E. Begin arm stretching exercises above the head right away.
A, C Strong magnetic fields can interfere with device function, and daily temperature checks help detect infection early.
B, D, E Others can feel an ICD shock but it is not harmful; tight clothing can irritate the site, and arm movement is restricted until healing and lead stability improve.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 30

Invasive Cardiovascular Procedures

Invasive cardiovascular procedures improve blood flow when noninvasive management is not enough. PCI opens coronary arteries, CABG bypasses obstructed coronary vessels, and peripheral bypass grafting restores limb perfusion, so nursing priorities are perfusion, bleeding control, airway support, pain control, graft patency, renal protection, and complication detection.

TL;DR - Open the Vessel, Protect the Graft, Catch Bleeding Fast

After PCI, watch the access site, distal perfusion, chest pain, contrast reaction, kidney function, and signs of tamponade or retroperitoneal bleeding. After CABG, support airway and ventilation, monitor hemodynamics and chest-tube output, treat dysrhythmias, control pain, and prevent pulmonary and infection complications. After peripheral bypass, frequent limb checks catch graft occlusion and compartment syndrome early.

Bleeding and perfusion

  • For femoral access, keep the extremity straight as prescribed and monitor the insertion site and distal pulses.
  • Blood under the client after turning suggests access-site bleeding that needs immediate site assessment and pressure.
  • For peripheral bypass, delayed capillary refill, mottling, pulse change, sensory/motor change, or temperature change can signal graft occlusion.

CABG recovery

  • Teach coughing, deep breathing, splinting, incentive spirometry, and early activity before surgery when possible.
  • Chest tube drainage greater than expected can signal hemorrhage and should be reported.
  • Pain control supports coughing, ventilation, and lower sympathetic stress.

Invasive Cardiovascular Procedure Care Map

Percutaneous Coronary Intervention

Open coronary flow

Purpose and indications

  • PCI is a nonsurgical method to open coronary arteries using atherectomy, angioplasty, stent placement, or a combination.
  • It can be elective for accessible coronary artery disease or time-sensitive during acute MI to restore perfusion.
  • Client findings can include chest pain with or without exertion, radiation to jaw/left arm/back/shoulder, dyspnea, nausea, fatigue, diaphoresis, ECG changes, blood pressure changes, vomiting, or confusion.

Complications

  • Artery dissection can cause severe hypotension and tachycardia and can require balloon occlusion, anticoagulant reversal, or emergency surgery.
  • Cardiac tamponade can cause hypotension, jugular venous distention, muffled heart sounds, pulsus paradoxus, elevated plateau pressures, and decreased cardiac output.
  • Monitor for hematoma, external bleeding, embolism, contrast allergy, retroperitoneal bleeding, restenosis, and acute kidney injury.

Coronary Artery Bypass Grafting

Revascularization

Why CABG is used

  • CABG restores myocardial blood flow by bypassing one or more obstructed coronary arteries.
  • Indications include significant left main coronary blockage with angina, multi-vessel disease, persistent ischemia after angiography/PCI/stent placement, acute MI with cardiogenic shock when appropriate, inaccessible lesions, and disease not responding to medical management.
  • Older adults are at increased risk for transient neurologic changes, medication toxicity, and dysrhythmias.

Before and during

  • Verify consent, confirm recent chest x-ray/ECG/labs if needed, and document baseline cognition, comorbidities, and support system.
  • Teach coughing, deep breathing, splinting, arm/leg exercises, pain reporting, tubes/lines, ventilator expectations, early ambulation, and medication changes.
  • During traditional CABG, a graft is connected past the occlusion; cardiopulmonary bypass can lower oxygen demand, stop heart motion, and later rewarm the client.

CABG Postprocedure Priorities

ICU recovery

Airway, rhythm, and hemodynamics

  • Maintain airway and ventilation; monitor respiratory effort, breath sounds, oxygen saturation, ventilator settings, and need for suction/extubation support.
  • Continuously monitor heart rate/rhythm and treat dysrhythmias per protocol.
  • Monitor blood pressure, hemodynamic pressures, catheter position/waveforms, level of consciousness, neurologic status, and urine output.
  • Hypotension can collapse grafts, while hypertension can increase bleeding from grafts and sutures.

Chest tubes, pain, and infection

  • Keep chest tubes patent, measure drainage at least hourly, report drainage above expected limits, and prevent dependent loops.
  • Differentiate radiating anginal pain from localized incisional pain; give adequate analgesia because uncontrolled pain increases heart rate and vascular resistance.
  • Monitor fluid/electrolytes, especially potassium and magnesium, and use sterile technique during dressing changes and suctioning.
  • Promote coughing, deep breathing, incentive spirometry, turning, early out-of-bed activity, and cardiac rehab planning.

Peripheral Bypass Grafts

Limb perfusion

Assessment clues

  • Peripheral bypass restores blood flow around an occluded peripheral artery; if circulation cannot be restored, amputation can be needed.
  • Expected PAD findings include intermittent claudication, rest pain relieved by dependency, decreased or absent foot pulses, cold/darkened skin, rubor when dependent, thick toenails, and arterial ulcers.
  • Before surgery, document baseline vital signs and peripheral pulses, verify consent, assess allergies, and teach NPO status, leg crossing avoidance, pain control, and breathing exercises.

After surgery

  • Assess vital signs and the operative limb frequently; monitor incision bleeding and distal pulses, capillary refill, color, temperature, sensation, and movement.
  • Maintain blood pressure in the prescribed range because hypotension can decrease graft flow and hypertension can promote bleeding.
  • Administer analgesics, antibiotics, anticoagulants, and antiplatelet therapy as prescribed.
  • Maintain bed rest with the leg straight initially, then progress ambulation, avoid prolonged sitting, and teach smoking cessation plus foot care.

High-Risk Complications

Report fast

After CABG

  • Pulmonary complications include atelectasis, pneumonia, and pulmonary edema; turn, suction, mobilize, and monitor respiratory status.
  • Hypothermia can cause vasoconstriction, metabolic acidosis, and hypertension; warm the client and monitor blood pressure.
  • Decreased cardiac output can come from dysrhythmias, tamponade, hypovolemia, left ventricular failure, or MI.
  • Assess neurologic status because blood pressure instability or clotting can cause neurologic deficits.

After peripheral bypass

  • Graft occlusion is most likely early after the procedure; report pulse, color, or temperature changes immediately and prepare for thrombectomy or thrombolysis if prescribed.
  • Compartment syndrome presents with worsening pain, swelling, and taut skin and can require fasciotomy.
  • Surgical-site infection can threaten graft survival; monitor redness, swelling, drainage, WBC count, temperature, and culture needs.
  • Teach the client to report decreased sensation, increased ischemic pain, redness, or swelling in the incision or affected limb.

Active Learning Scenario

Therapeutic Procedure - Angioplasty Postprocedure Care

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse develops a plan of care for a client returning to the unit after angioplasty.

Nursing interventions

  • Assess vital signs and the access site at ordered intervals.
  • Maintain prescribed bed rest, supine positioning, and affected-leg straightness after femoral access.
  • Continue cardiac monitoring for dysrhythmias and assess for chest pain.
  • Administer antiplatelet, thrombolytic, anxiolytic, and analgesic medications as prescribed.
  • Monitor urine output and provide IV fluids to support contrast clearance and hydration.

Potential complications

  • Cardiac tamponade: notify the provider, support blood pressure with IV fluids, confirm findings, and prepare for urgent intervention.
  • Hematoma or external bleeding: assess distal perfusion, hold pressure for uncontrolled bleeding, keep the extremity straight, and notify the provider.
  • Allergic reaction, embolism, retroperitoneal bleeding, restenosis, and kidney injury require targeted monitoring and rapid provider notification.

Application Exercises

1

A client is 4 hr postoperative after CABG. The client inspires 200 mL with the incentive spirometer, then refuses to cough because of fatigue and pain. Which action should the nurse take?

  • A. Allow the client to rest and return in 1 hr.
  • B. Administer IV bolus analgesic and return in 15 min.
  • C. Document 200 mL as adequate inspired volume.
  • D. Tell the client that coughing is required.
B Adequate analgesia improves coughing effectiveness and reduces pulmonary complication risk.
A, C, D Delaying coughing, accepting a low volume, or using nontherapeutic direction does not address pain-limited ventilation.
2

A client is recovering from left lower-extremity peripheral bypass graft surgery. Which findings require immediate concern? Select all that apply.

  • A. Trace bloody drainage on the dressing.
  • B. Capillary refill of the affected limb of 6 seconds.
  • C. Mottled appearance of the affected limb.
  • D. Throbbing pain relieved by IV bolus analgesic.
  • E. Pulse 2+ in the affected limb.
B, C Delayed capillary refill and mottling can indicate impaired graft perfusion and should be reported promptly.
A, D, E Trace drainage, relieved throbbing pain from restored flow, and a 2+ pulse are not the immediate concern in this set.
3

Which statements about cardiopulmonary bypass during CABG are accurate? Select all that apply.

  • A. It lowers the client's oxygen demand.
  • B. It allows heart motion to stop for graft placement.
  • C. It includes rewarming after cooling when used.
  • D. It increases the client's metabolic rate.
  • E. It stops blood flow to the heart completely.
A, B, C Cardiopulmonary bypass can reduce oxygen demand, provide a motionless field, and rewarm the client after hypothermia.
D, E Metabolic rate is decreased, and the bypass machine maintains blood flow during the procedure.
4

After femoral angioplasty, the nurse turns the client and finds blood underneath the client's lower back. Which finding should the nurse suspect?

  • A. Retroperitoneal bleeding.
  • B. Cardiac tamponade.
  • C. Bleeding from the insertion site.
  • D. Heart failure.
C Visible blood draining under the client points to external insertion-site bleeding; assess the site, apply pressure, monitor, and notify the provider.

  • A. Rubor of the affected leg when elevated.
  • B. 3+ dorsalis pedis pulse in the affected foot.
  • C. Thin, peeling toenails of the affected foot.
  • D. Intermittent claudication in the affected leg.
D Peripheral artery disease commonly causes activity-related numbness or burning pain that improves with rest.
A, B, C Rubor appears when the limb is dependent, pulses are decreased or absent, and toenails are typically thickened.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 31

Angina and Myocardial Infarction

Acute coronary syndrome sits on a continuum from angina to myocardial infarction. Nursing priorities are rapid recognition of ischemic pain, serial ECG and cardiac-marker review, oxygen-demand reduction, medication safety, and fast escalation when pain is not relieved by rest or nitroglycerin.

TL;DR - Rest, Nitro, ECG, Markers, Reperfuse

Stable angina is predictable and improves with rest or nitroglycerin. MI pain lasts longer, is not relieved by rest or nitroglycerin, and can require opioids and reperfusion therapy. Monitor serial ECGs, cardiac enzymes, oxygenation, pain, rhythm, urine output, and bleeding risk from antiplatelet, anticoagulant, or thrombolytic therapy.

Differentiate the pain

  • Stable angina is triggered by exertion or stress and usually resolves in 15 min or less with rest or nitroglycerin.
  • Unstable angina increases in frequency, severity, or duration and can occur at rest.
  • MI pain often lasts more than 30 min and can include nausea, dyspnea, anxiety, diaphoresis, and epigastric distress.

Immediate priorities

  • Trend ECG changes: ST depression/T-wave inversion suggests ischemia, ST elevation suggests injury, and abnormal Q waves suggest necrosis.
  • Use cardiac markers to time injury: myoglobin clears early, CK-MB clears after about 3 days, troponin I lasts 7 to 10 days, and troponin T can last 10 to 14 days.
  • Smoking cessation is the first lifestyle priority because nicotine causes vasoconstriction and narrows coronary arteries.

Angina and MI Care Map

Acute Coronary Syndrome Recognition

Ischemia to infarction

Risk and presentation

  • Risk factors include hypertension, tobacco use, hyperlipidemia, obesity, diabetes mellitus, hyperthyroidism, sedentary lifestyle, stress with ineffective coping, and stimulant use such as cocaine or methamphetamine.
  • Chest pain can be substernal or precordial and may radiate to the shoulder, arm, jaw, neck, back, or shoulder.
  • Female clients can have atypical symptoms such as pain between the shoulders, jaw ache, or a choking sensation with exertion.
  • Older adults and female clients might not have classic angina or MI findings.

Assessment findings

  • Subjective findings can include impending doom, anxiety, pressure/crushing pain, nausea, and dizziness.
  • Physical findings can include pallor, cool clammy skin, tachycardia, palpitations, tachypnea, shortness of breath, diaphoresis, vomiting, and decreased level of consciousness.
  • Stable angina is relieved by rest and nitroglycerin; MI is usually relieved only with opioid analgesia and reperfusion-focused care.

Diagnostics and Monitoring

Trend changes

Markers and ECG

  • Myoglobin rises early after cardiac or skeletal muscle injury and is no longer evident after about 24 hr.
  • CK-MB peaks around 24 hr after chest-pain onset and is no longer evident after about 3 days.
  • Any positive troponin I or T indicates cardiac tissue damage and should be reported.
  • Serial ECGs help distinguish ischemia, injury, and necrosis through ST/T/Q-wave changes.

Procedure support

  • Stress testing evaluates response to increased heart rate through exercise or medication.
  • Thallium scan identifies areas of ischemia or necrosis as decreased or absent perfusion.
  • Cardiac catheterization/coronary angiography evaluates location and degree of coronary blockage; verify consent, NPO status, and contrast allergy screening.
  • Monitor vital signs, serial ECGs, continuous rhythm, pain details, urine output, cardiac enzymes, electrolytes, and ABGs.

Medication Priorities

Oxygen demand and clot control

Oxygen demand

  • Nitroglycerin decreases preload and afterload and helps prevent coronary vasospasm; check for recent phosphodiesterase inhibitor use because severe hypotension can occur.
  • Teach chest-pain response: stop activity, rest, place nitroglycerin under the tongue, call emergency services if unrelieved after 5 min, and take up to two more doses 5 min apart if directed.
  • Morphine treats moderate to severe pain and reduces myocardial oxygen consumption; monitor respirations, blood pressure, nausea, vomiting, and sedation.
  • Beta blockers such as metoprolol slow heart rate and reduce afterload; monitor for bradycardia, hypotension, decreased LOC, crackles, and chest discomfort.

Clot control

  • Thrombolytics such as alteplase and reteplase break up clots and work best when given early after infarction; assess bleeding contraindications first.
  • Aspirin and clopidogrel reduce platelet aggregation; teach bruising/bleeding risk, GI protection, and tinnitus reporting for aspirin toxicity.
  • Heparin and enoxaparin keep existing clots from enlarging and prevent additional clots; monitor platelet count and coagulation studies.
  • Glycoprotein IIb/IIIa inhibitors can be used with aspirin therapy and require platelet and bleeding surveillance.

Complications After Ischemic Injury

Pump and rhythm failure

Decreased output

  • Acute MI is a complication of angina not relieved by rest or nitroglycerin; administer oxygen to maintain saturation at least 90% and notify the provider.
  • Left ventricular injury can lead to heart failure and cardiogenic shock with tachycardia, hypotension, low urine output, altered LOC, respiratory distress, cool clammy skin, decreased pulses, and chest pain.
  • Treatment can require oxygen, intubation/ventilation, morphine, diuretics, nitroglycerin, vasopressors, inotropes, and continuous hemodynamic monitoring.

Structural and rhythm changes

  • A new murmur can signal ischemic mitral regurgitation and requires immediate provider notification.
  • Ventricular aneurysm or rupture can cause sudden chest pain, dysrhythmias, and severe hypotension.
  • Inferior MI can injure the AV node and cause bradycardia or second-degree AV block.
  • Anterior MI can injure the ventricle and cause PVCs, bundle branch block, or complete heart block; prepare for pacing or ICD support if needed.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Acute Coronary Syndrome

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse teaches a client with a new diagnosis of angina about acute coronary syndrome and risk reduction.

Risk factors and findings

  • Risk factors include male sex or postmenopause, sedentary lifestyle, hypertension, tobacco/stimulant/excess alcohol use, hyperlipidemia, metabolic disorders, and poorly managed stress.
  • Subjective findings can include chest pressure or crushing pain radiating to the arm or jaw, impending doom, nausea, dizziness, and anxiety.
  • Physical findings can include pale cool clammy skin, tachycardia, tachypnea, and diaphoresis.

Teaching

  • Schedule routine blood pressure, cholesterol, and blood-glucose checks.
  • Use regular physical activity as prescribed for exercise and stress reduction.
  • Stop smoking and avoid stimulant substances that increase vasoconstriction and cardiac workload.

Application Exercises

1

Which finding helps distinguish stable angina from myocardial infarction?

  • A. Stable angina is relieved with rest and nitroglycerin.
  • B. MI pain resolves in less than 15 min.
  • C. MI always follows an identifiable activity.
  • D. Stable angina usually lasts longer than 30 min.
A Stable angina is typically relieved by rest and nitroglycerin.
B, C, D MI pain usually lasts longer than 30 min and can occur after rest; stable angina usually lasts 15 min or less.
2

A client reports dyspnea that began 2 weeks ago and has a suspected prior MI. Which marker can still support MI 10 to 14 days after injury?

  • A. CK-MB.
  • B. Troponin I.
  • C. Troponin T.
  • D. Myoglobin.
C Troponin T can remain evident for 10 to 14 days after MI.
A, B, D Myoglobin clears within about 24 hr, CK-MB after about 3 days, and troponin I after about 7 to 10 days.
3

A client asks why daily aspirin was prescribed after coronary symptoms. Which response should the nurse make?

  • A. "Aspirin reduces formation of clots that could cause a heart attack."
  • B. "Aspirin relieves myocardial ischemic pain."
  • C. "Aspirin dissolves coronary artery clots."
  • D. "Aspirin prevents headaches from other cardiac medications."
A Aspirin decreases platelet aggregation that can contribute to arterial clotting and MI.
B, C, D Daily aspirin is not adequate ischemic-pain relief, does not dissolve clots, and is not prescribed here for headache prevention.
4

Which statement by a client taking metoprolol for angina indicates understanding?

  • A. "I should place the tablet under my tongue."
  • B. "I should have my clotting time checked weekly."
  • C. "I will report ringing in my ears."
  • D. "I will call my provider if my pulse is less than 60/min."
D Metoprolol can cause bradycardia; the client should report a pulse below the prescribed parameter.
A, B, C Metoprolol is oral, does not require weekly clotting-time checks, and tinnitus is linked with aspirin toxicity rather than metoprolol.
5

During community teaching about preventing angina and MI, which lifestyle change should the nurse recommend first?

  • A. Modify diet.
  • B. Use relaxation exercises.
  • C. Stop smoking.
  • D. Take omega-3 capsules.
C Smoking cessation is first because nicotine causes vasoconstriction, elevates blood pressure, and narrows coronary arteries.
A, B, D Diet, stress reduction, and omega-3 intake can help, but smoking cessation is the first priority in this set.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 32

Heart Failure and Pulmonary Edema

Heart failure occurs when the heart cannot maintain adequate circulation to meet tissue needs, causing inadequate cardiac output and pulmonary or systemic congestion. Pulmonary edema is the life-threatening end of this problem, requiring rapid positioning, oxygenation, diuresis, preload/afterload reduction, and close output monitoring.

TL;DR - Sit Up, Oxygenate, Diurese, Trend Weight

Left-sided HF causes poor tissue perfusion and pulmonary congestion; right-sided HF causes systemic venous congestion and edema. For acute pulmonary edema, place the client in high-Fowler's with legs dependent, give high-flow oxygen or positive airway support, restrict fluids, monitor urine output, and give rapid diuretics and cardiac-output medications as prescribed.

Heart failure clues

  • Left-sided findings include dyspnea, orthopnea, nocturnal dyspnea, fatigue, pulmonary congestion, S3, frothy sputum, altered mental status, and oliguria.
  • Right-sided findings include jugular venous distention, dependent edema, ascites, hepatomegaly, nausea, anorexia, nocturia, and weight gain.
  • BNP elevation helps distinguish heart failure from respiratory causes of dyspnea and guides treatment intensity.

Home safety

  • Weigh daily at the same time and report a gain of more than 2 lb in 24 hr or 5 lb in 1 week.
  • Follow sodium and fluid restrictions and continue medications even when feeling better.
  • Report shortness of breath, angina, swelling of feet or ankles, and medication-toxicity findings.

Heart Failure and Pulmonary Edema Care Map

Types, Staging, and Assessment

Pump failure

Classification

  • NYHA class I has no activity symptoms; class II has symptoms with ordinary exertion; class III with minimal exertion; class IV at rest.
  • ACC/AHA stage A is high risk, stage B has structural changes without symptoms, stage C has current or prior symptoms, and stage D is refractory end-stage HF.
  • Systolic left-sided failure has ejection fraction below 40% with pulmonary and systemic congestion; diastolic failure has stiff or poorly relaxing ventricular filling.
  • Right-sided failure produces systemic venous congestion and peripheral edema.

Risk and findings

  • Left-sided HF risk factors include hypertension, coronary artery disease, angina, MI, and mitral/aortic valve disease.
  • Right-sided HF can follow left-sided HF, right ventricular MI, COPD, or pulmonary fibrosis.
  • High-output HF can occur with increased metabolic needs, sepsis, anemia, or hyperthyroidism.
  • Older adults can have masked HF findings when lung disease or kidney failure is also present.

Diagnostics and Ongoing Nursing Care

Trend congestion

Diagnostics

  • Hemodynamic monitoring in HF generally shows increased CVP, PAWP, and PAP with decreased cardiac output.
  • A drop in mixed venous oxygen saturation can indicate worsening cardiac function.
  • Echocardiography evaluates systolic and diastolic function; expected left ventricular ejection fraction is 55% to 70%.
  • Chest x-ray can show cardiomegaly and pleural effusions; ECG, enzymes, electrolytes, and ABGs help identify contributing factors and impact.

Nursing care

  • Monitor daily weight, intake and output, dyspnea, vital signs, hemodynamic pressures, diagnostic trends, and medication toxicity.
  • Position in high-Fowler's to maximize ventilation and administer oxygen as prescribed.
  • Encourage bed rest until stable, cluster care, support ADLs, and maintain prescribed fluid and sodium restrictions.
  • Provide emotional support and include family in teaching and discharge planning.

Medication Priorities

Preload, afterload, contractility

Fluid and resistance control

  • Loop, thiazide, and potassium-sparing diuretics decrease preload; give IV furosemide no faster than 20 mg/min.
  • Loop and thiazide diuretics can cause hypokalemia; potassium-sparing diuretics require monitoring for hidden potassium sources such as salt substitutes.
  • ACE inhibitors, ARBs, calcium-channel blockers, and phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitors reduce afterload and help the heart pump more easily.
  • ACE inhibitors can cause first-dose hypotension, hyperkalemia, dry cough, rash, altered taste, and angioedema.

Output and rhythm support

  • Inotropes such as digoxin, dopamine, dobutamine, milrinone, and levosimendan improve contractility and cardiac output.
  • Before digoxin, check apical pulse for 1 min and hold for pulse below prescribed limits, commonly below 50 to 60/min.
  • Digoxin teaching includes taking it at the same time daily, separating antacids by at least 2 hr, and reporting fatigue, weakness, confusion, or poor appetite.
  • Beta blockers can improve chronic HF status; monitor blood pressure, pulse, activity tolerance, orthopnea, and orthostatic readings.

Advanced Therapies and Teaching

End-stage support

VAD and transplant

  • A ventricular assist device is a mechanical pump for clients awaiting transplant or with severe end-stage HF who are not transplant candidates.
  • VAD surgery is contraindicated with severe chronic lung disease, end-stage kidney disease, clotting disorders, or infections unresponsive to antibiotics.
  • Heart transplant can be considered for end-stage HF and requires post-transplant immunosuppressant therapy.
  • Transplant recipients are at risk for infection, thrombosis, and rejection.

Discharge teaching

  • Take diuretics in the early morning and early afternoon.
  • Restrict fluid and sodium as instructed and regulate potassium intake according to the medication plan.
  • Use a daily weight routine and accurate fluid-measurement strategy, such as tracking intake in an empty 2 L bottle when prescribed.
  • Keep follow-up visits and obtain recommended pneumococcal and yearly influenza vaccines.

Acute Pulmonary Edema

Medical emergency

Expected findings

  • Pulmonary edema is life-threatening fluid accumulation in alveoli and interstitial lung spaces, most often from severe cardiac dysfunction.
  • Findings include anxiety, inability to sleep, tachypnea, dyspnea, orthopnea, hypoxemia, crackles, tachycardia, reduced urine output, confusion, S3, and increased PAOP.
  • Persistent cough with pink frothy sputum is a key finding.
  • Older adults have increased risk and are vulnerable to fluid/electrolyte imbalance during diuretic therapy.

Emergency care

  • Position high-Fowler's with feet and legs dependent or sitting on the side of the bed to decrease preload.
  • Administer high-flow oxygen by mask or nonrebreather; be ready for bilevel positive airway pressure, intubation, and ventilation.
  • Monitor vital signs every 15 min until stable, intake/output, hemodynamics, ABGs, potassium, oxygen saturation, and chest x-ray findings.
  • Restrict fluids, monitor hourly urine output, suction as needed, give rapid-acting loop diuretics, morphine, vasodilators, inotropes, and antihypertensives as prescribed.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Heart Failure

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A cardiac rehabilitation nurse teaches a class on heart failure.

Alteration and diagnostics

  • Left-sided HF causes inadequate left-ventricular output and poor tissue perfusion; right-sided HF causes systemic venous congestion and peripheral edema.
  • BNP helps confirm HF in a client with dyspnea and guides treatment intensity.
  • Useful diagnostics include hemodynamic monitoring, ultrasound/echocardiography, chest x-ray, and ECG.

Medication groups

  • Diuretics include furosemide, bumetanide, hydrochlorothiazide, and spironolactone.
  • Afterload-reducing agents include enalapril, captopril, losartan, diltiazem, nifedipine, and milrinone.
  • Inotropic therapy such as digoxin or IV inotropes supports contractility and cardiac output when indicated.

Application Exercises

1

A client with heart failure reports increased shortness of breath. Which action should the nurse take first?

  • A. Obtain the client's weight.
  • B. Assist the client into high-Fowler's position.
  • C. Auscultate lung sounds.
  • D. Check oxygen saturation.
B Using ABC priorities, high-Fowler's decreases venous return and helps relieve pulmonary congestion first.
A, C, D Weight, lung sounds, and oxygen saturation matter, but positioning is the immediate priority for worsening dyspnea.
2

Which teaching should the nurse include for a client with heart failure who has new prescriptions for furosemide and digoxin? Select all that apply.

  • A. Weigh daily first thing each morning.
  • B. Decrease potassium intake while taking furosemide.
  • C. Report muscle weakness while taking digoxin.
  • D. Hold digoxin if heart rate is less than 70/min.
  • E. Decrease sodium intake.
A, C, E Daily weight tracks fluid status, muscle weakness can indicate digoxin toxicity, and sodium restriction helps prevent fluid retention.
B, D Furosemide can require potassium intake/supplement support; digoxin hold parameters are prescribed, commonly below 50 to 60/min rather than automatically below 70/min.
3

Which findings should the nurse expect with pulmonary edema? Select all that apply.

  • A. Tachypnea.
  • B. Persistent cough.
  • C. Increased urinary output.
  • D. Thick yellow sputum.
  • E. Orthopnea.
A, B, E Tachypnea, persistent cough with pink frothy sputum, and orthopnea are expected pulmonary edema findings.
C, D Decreased urine output is expected; sputum is classically pink and frothy rather than thick yellow.
4

A client with class I heart failure asks about a ventricular assist device. Which response should the nurse make?

  • A. "VADs are only implanted during heart transplantation."
  • B. "A VAD helps pace the heart."
  • C. "VADs are used when heart failure is not responsive to medications."
  • D. "A VAD is useful for clients who also have chronic lung disease."
C A VAD can prolong life in severe HF that is not responsive to medication therapy.
A, B, D VADs can bridge to transplant but are not only implanted during transplant, they pump rather than pace, and severe chronic lung disease is a contraindication.
5

A client with heart failure has a fluid restriction of 2,000 mL/day and asks how to track intake. Which response is best?

  • A. "Pour the amount you drink into an empty 2 L bottle to track intake."
  • B. "You can have 8 glasses because each glass is 8 oz."
  • C. "This is about the same as two pots of coffee."
  • D. "Take sips of water or ice chips so you will not take in too much."
A Using an empty 2 L bottle gives a visual, measurable guide for daily fluid intake.
B, C, D Cup sizes vary, vague comparisons are inaccurate, and sipping/ice chips do not provide a reliable measurement plan.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 33

Valvular Heart Disease

Valvular heart disease occurs when stenosis or insufficiency disrupts forward blood flow and reduces stroke volume. Nursing care focuses on murmur recognition, heart-failure surveillance, anticoagulation and infection-prevention teaching, and knowing when valve procedures are used to improve function or quality of life.

TL;DR - Hear the Murmur, Watch Failure, Protect the Valve

Stenosis blocks forward flow; insufficiency lets blood leak backward. Left-sided valve disease can cause pulmonary congestion, orthopnea, PND, fatigue, and decreased cardiac output. Mechanical valves require lifelong anticoagulation, invasive dental/respiratory procedures can require prophylactic antibiotics, and sudden weight gain or fever needs prompt reporting.

Core differences

  • Stenosis is a narrowed valve opening that impedes forward blood flow.
  • Insufficiency or regurgitation means the valve closes poorly and blood flows backward.
  • Valve damage reduces pump efficiency, reduces stroke volume, and can progress to hypertrophy and heart failure.

Must-teach items

  • Weigh daily and report a gain of 3 lb in 1 day or 5 lb in 1 week.
  • Report heart-failure findings, fever, or petechial rash right away.
  • Read over-the-counter labels and avoid products with alcohol, ephedrine, or epinephrine unless cleared.

Valvular Heart Disease Care Map

Cause, Prevention, and Assessment

Stenosis vs regurgitation

Risk and prevention

  • Risk factors include hypertension, rheumatic fever, infective endocarditis, congenital malformations, Marfan syndrome, degenerative calcification, atherosclerosis, and papillary muscle dysfunction.
  • Prevent and treat bacterial infections early, especially streptococcal infections that can contribute to rheumatic disease.
  • Control diabetes, hypertension, and hypercholesterolemia.
  • Use low-sodium diet and prescribed fluid restrictions to reduce heart-failure strain.

Findings

  • Many clients are asymptomatic until late in disease progression.
  • Murmur timing and location help identify the involved valve; murmurs are graded from very faint to extremely loud.
  • Left-sided valve damage can cause pulmonary artery pressure elevation, left ventricular hypertrophy, decreased output, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, and fatigue.
  • Right-sided disease can include dyspnea, fatigue, irregular rhythm, and peripheral edema.

Left-Sided Valve Patterns

Murmur clues

Mitral valve

  • Mitral stenosis can produce an apical diastolic murmur, dyspnea on exertion, orthopnea, atrial fibrillation, palpitations, fatigue, JVD, pitting edema, hemoptysis, dry cough, PND, and hepatomegaly.
  • Mitral insufficiency can produce a systolic apical murmur, S3, fatigue, weakness, atrial fibrillation, exertional dyspnea, orthopnea, atypical chest pain, palpitations, JVD, pitting edema, PND, and hepatomegaly.
  • Neck vein distention can be expected with pulmonary congestion from mitral insufficiency.

Aortic valve

  • Aortic stenosis can cause a systolic murmur, exertional dyspnea, S4, angina, syncope with exertion, fatigue, orthopnea, PND, and narrowed pulse pressure.
  • Aortic insufficiency can cause a diastolic murmur, sinus tachycardia, exertional dyspnea, orthopnea, palpitations, fatigue, nocturnal angina with diaphoresis, widened pulse pressure, and bounding arterial pulse.
  • Chest x-ray, ECG, echocardiography, TEE, stress echocardiography, radionuclide studies, angiography, and cardiac catheterization can help define severity and cardiac effect.

Medication and Procedure Priorities

Failure prevention

Medications

  • Diuretics treat heart failure by removing excess extracellular fluid; monitor for hypokalemia with loop and thiazide agents.
  • Afterload-reducing agents help the heart pump against less resistance; monitor for first-dose hypotension with ACE inhibitors.
  • Digoxin can improve cardiac output; teach 1-min pulse checks, same-time dosing, antacid separation by at least 2 hr, and toxicity reporting.
  • Anticoagulation is used for mechanical valve replacement, atrial fibrillation, or severe left ventricular dysfunction.

Procedures

  • Percutaneous balloon valvuloplasty can open stenotic aortic or mitral valves by improving leaflet mobility.
  • Valve replacement can use mechanical valves, xenografts, allografts, or autografts.
  • Mechanical valves require lifelong anticoagulation; tissue valves generally require later replacement.
  • Other surgical options include commissurotomy, annuloplasty ring insertion, chordae tendineae reconstruction, and leaflet repair.

Postprocedure and Home Teaching

Prevent complications

After surgery

  • Post-valve surgery care is similar to CABG care, including sternal incision care and activity limitation during early recovery.
  • Coordinate activity with planned rest periods and use energy conservation.
  • Follow prescribed exercise, dietary, sodium, caffeine, and alcohol restrictions.
  • For mechanical valves, keep regular anticoagulation monitoring appointments.

Infection and HF alerts

  • Use prophylactic antibiotics before invasive dental or respiratory procedures when prescribed.
  • Clean open wounds carefully and use antibiotic ointment as directed.
  • Report fever, petechial rash, or manifestations of heart failure immediately.
  • Interprofessional support can include respiratory, cardiology, nutrition, and rehabilitation services.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Valvular Heart Disease

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse educator prepares a health-fair poster on valvular heart disease.

Alteration in health

  • Stenosis narrows a valve opening and prevents forward flow.
  • Insufficiency is poor closure that allows backward regurgitation.
  • Congenital disease can involve any valve; acquired disease can be degenerative, rheumatic, or infective.

Client education

  • Prevent and manage hypertension.
  • Seek early treatment for bacterial infections.
  • Follow a low-sodium diet and prescribed activity plan.

Application Exercises

1

Which statement by a client with a new mechanical heart valve indicates understanding of discharge teaching?

  • A. "I can return to my usual exercise routine right away."
  • B. "I will have my prothrombin time checked regularly."
  • C. "I no longer need antibiotics before dental work."
  • D. "I will continue to limit potassium-containing foods."
B Mechanical valves require anticoagulation, so regular coagulation monitoring is expected.
A, C, D Activity is limited during early recovery, antibiotic prophylaxis can be needed before dental work, and sodium rather than potassium is the key routine dietary restriction here.
2

Which finding should the nurse expect in a client who has mitral valve insufficiency?

  • A. S4 heart sound.
  • B. Petechiae.
  • C. Neck vein distention.
  • D. Splenomegaly.
C Neck vein distention can occur with pulmonary congestion related to mitral valve insufficiency.
A, B, D S4 is associated with aortic stenosis, petechiae with infective endocarditis, and hepatomegaly rather than splenomegaly is expected with left-sided valve damage.
3

Which data are risk factors for valvular heart disease? Select all that apply.

  • A. Surgical repair of an atrial septal defect at age 2.
  • B. Measles infection during childhood.
  • C. Hypertension for 5 years.
  • D. Weight gain of 10 lb in the past year.
  • E. Diastolic murmur present.
A, C, E Congenital malformation history, hypertension, and a murmur from turbulent flow are associated with valvular heart disease.
B, D Rheumatic fever or streptococcal infection, not measles, is the infectious risk; sudden weight gain can reflect fluid retention but is not the risk factor in this item.
4

An older adult is scheduled for percutaneous balloon valvuloplasty. Which outcome should the nurse explain to the family?

  • A. It improves blood flow in the coronary arteries.
  • B. It can improve ability to perform activities of daily living.
  • C. It reliably prolongs lifespan with the valve disorder.
  • D. It reverses damage already present in the valve.
B Valvuloplasty can improve valve flow and quality of life when symptoms interfere with daily activities.
A, C, D It opens valve commissures rather than coronary arteries, does not necessarily prolong life, and does not reverse existing valve damage.
5

  • A. Dyspnea.
  • B. Fatigue.
  • C. Bradycardia.
  • D. Pleural friction rub.
  • E. Peripheral edema.
A, B, E Dyspnea, fatigue, and peripheral edema can be manifestations of right-sided valvular disease.

Unit 4 · Cardiovascular Disorders · Chapter 34

Inflammatory Disorders

Cardiac inflammatory disorders can involve the pericardium, myocardium, or endocardium and can destroy healthy tissue if infection or inflammation is not controlled. Nursing priorities are early infection treatment, rhythm and oxygenation monitoring, medication safety, antibiotic adherence, and rapid recognition of cardiac tamponade.

TL;DR - Treat Infection, Rest the Heart, Watch Tamponade

Pericarditis pain worsens with inspiration, coughing, or swallowing and improves when sitting forward; myocarditis can cause tachycardia, dysrhythmias, and cardiomegaly; rheumatic endocarditis follows untreated streptococcal pharyngitis; infective endocarditis can show fever, murmur, petechiae, positive blood cultures, and splinter hemorrhages. Tamponade is the emergency to catch fast.

Prevent

  • Treat streptococcal infections early to prevent rheumatic fever.
  • Use prophylactic antibiotics for at-risk cardiac defects when prescribed.
  • Keep influenza and pneumonia immunizations current to lower myocarditis-related infection risk.

Escalate

  • Notify the provider for ECG changes, fever, heart-failure findings, or tamponade manifestations.
  • Cardiac tamponade can cause dyspnea, dizziness, chest tightness, restlessness, pulsus paradoxus, tachycardia, muffled heart sounds, and JVD.
  • Prepare for pericardiocentesis when tamponade is suspected.

Inflammatory Cardiac Disorder Care Map

Disorder Patterns

Layer of the heart

Pericarditis and myocarditis

  • Pericarditis is inflammation of the pericardium and commonly follows respiratory infection, MI, or systemic connective-tissue disease flare.
  • Pericarditis findings include chest pressure or pain worsened by inspiration, coughing, or swallowing; friction rub at the left lower sternal border; shortness of breath; and relief when sitting and leaning forward.
  • Myocarditis is inflammation of the myocardium from infection or systemic inflammatory disease.
  • Myocarditis findings include tachycardia, murmur, lung friction rub, cardiomegaly, chest pain, and dysrhythmias.

Endocarditis

  • Rheumatic endocarditis follows group A beta-hemolytic streptococcal pharyngitis and can cause fever, chest pain, joint pain, tachycardia, shortness of breath, rash, friction rub, murmur, and muscle spasms.
  • Infective endocarditis is infection of the endocardium from organisms such as staphylococci, streptococci, or fungi.
  • Risk is higher with structural cardiac malformations, cardiac devices, prosthetic valves, IV substance use, and invasive procedures that cause bacteremia.
  • Findings include fever, flu-like symptoms, murmur, petechiae, positive blood cultures, and splinter hemorrhages.

Diagnostics and Nursing Care

Infection and rhythm

Tests

  • Blood cultures can identify bacterial infection and guide antimicrobial therapy.
  • WBC elevation can suggest bacterial infection, and elevated ESR/CRP supports systemic inflammation.
  • Cardiac enzymes can be elevated with pericarditis.
  • ECG can show heart block with rheumatic fever or widespread ST elevation with pericarditis; echocardiography can show inflamed layers or pericardial effusion.

Nursing care

  • Auscultate for murmur or friction rub and review ABGs, oxygen saturation, and chest x-ray results.
  • Administer oxygen, monitor vital signs and fever, monitor ECG, and report changes.
  • Monitor for cardiac tamponade and heart failure.
  • Obtain throat cultures when indicated, administer antibiotics and antipyretics, assess pain, give pain medication, encourage bed rest, and support coping.

Medications and Procedures

Treat cause

Medications

  • Penicillin treats infection; monitor for rash, hives, electrolyte levels, and kidney function, and teach GI distress reporting.
  • NSAIDs such as ibuprofen treat fever and inflammation with pericarditis; avoid in peptic ulcer disease and monitor GI effects, platelets, liver function, and kidney function.
  • Prednisone treats inflammation; use low doses, monitor blood pressure, electrolytes, blood glucose, and impaired healing, and teach not to stop abruptly.
  • Amphotericin B treats fungal infection; monitor liver and kidney function.

Procedures and follow-up

  • Pericardiocentesis aspirates pericardial fluid and can send fluid for culture and sensitivity.
  • After pericardiocentesis, monitor for recurrence of tamponade.
  • In infective endocarditis, procedures can include valve debridement, abscess drainage, or congenital shunt repair.
  • Monitor after procedures for bleeding, infection, and altered cardiac output.

Cardiac Tamponade

Emergency

Recognize it

  • Tamponade can result from fluid accumulation in the pericardial sac.
  • Findings include dyspnea, dizziness, chest tightness, increasing restlessness, pulsus paradoxus, tachycardia, muffled heart sounds, and jugular venous distention.
  • Hemodynamic monitoring can show similar elevated intracardiac and pulmonary artery plateau pressures.

Respond fast

  • Notify the provider immediately and support blood pressure with IV fluids as prescribed.
  • Obtain chest x-ray or echocardiogram to confirm the diagnosis.
  • Prepare for pericardiocentesis, including consent, supplies, and prescribed medications.
  • Monitor hemodynamics, rhythm changes, and recurrence of findings after the procedure.

Active Learning Scenario

System Disorder - Myocarditis Discharge Teaching

ATI-supported ALS

Scenario: A nurse reviews discharge teaching with a client who has myocarditis.

Referral resources

  • Home health can support postoperative care and home IV antibiotic therapy.
  • Pharmaceutical services can provide IV therapy supplies and medications.
  • Rehabilitation services can help monitor and gradually increase activity.

Home actions

  • Rest as needed and take medications as prescribed.
  • Wash hands, maintain good oral hygiene, and avoid crowded areas to reduce infection risk.
  • Learn IV antibiotic administration and management if prescribed for home therapy.
  • Participate in tobacco-use cessation if applicable.

Application Exercises

1

Which finding should the nurse expect in a client who has pericarditis?

  • A. Petechiae.
  • B. Murmur.
  • C. Rash.
  • D. Friction rub.
D A pericardial friction rub is expected with pericarditis.
A, B, C Petechiae point toward endocarditis, murmur toward myocarditis or endocarditis, and rash toward rheumatic endocarditis.
2

Which client is at risk for developing rheumatic endocarditis?

  • A. Older adult with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.
  • B. Child with streptococcal pharyngitis.
  • C. Middle-aged adult with lupus erythematosus.
  • D. Young adult who recently received a body tattoo.
B Streptococcal pharyngitis can progress to rheumatic fever, which can lead to rheumatic endocarditis.
A, C, D COPD and lupus are not the specific rheumatic-endocarditis risk unless rheumatic fever occurs; tattooing increases infective endocarditis risk.
3

A client has been taking long-term NSAID therapy for pericarditis. Which laboratory finding should the nurse report?

  • A. Platelets 100,000/mm3.
  • B. Serum glucose 110 mg/dL.
  • C. Serum creatinine 0.7 mg/dL.
  • D. ALT 30 IU/L.
A Long-term NSAID therapy can lower platelets; 100,000/mm3 is outside expected range and should be reported.
B, C, D The listed glucose, creatinine, and ALT values are within expected ranges for this item.
4

Splinter hemorrhages under the nail beds plus fever are associated with which disorder?

  • A. Infective endocarditis.
  • B. Pericarditis.
  • C. Myocarditis.
  • D. Rheumatic endocarditis.
A Fever and splinter hemorrhages are classic manifestations of infective endocarditis.
B, C, D Pericarditis is associated with chest pain/friction rub, myocarditis with rapid heart rate, and rheumatic endocarditis with joint pain and preceding streptococcal infection.
5

Which laboratory test helps confirm suspected rheumatic endocarditis?

  • A. Arterial blood gases.
  • B. Serum albumin.
  • C. Liver enzymes.
  • D. Throat culture.
D Throat culture can identify streptococcal infection, the leading cause of rheumatic endocarditis.
A, B, C ABGs, albumin, and liver enzymes can support broader assessment but do not confirm the streptococcal source.
